# Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents

![[Uncaptioned
image]](2610.29357v1/figures/institutions/hit.jpg)   ![[Uncaptioned
image]](2610.29357v1/figures/institutions/slai.png)   ![[Uncaptioned
image]](2610.29357v1/figures/institutions/tsinghua.png)   ![[Uncaptioned
image]](2610.29357v1/figures/institutions/seu.png)   ![[Uncaptioned
image]](2610.29357v1/figures/institutions/china-telecom.png)   ![[Uncaptioned
image]](2610.29357v1/figures/institutions/huawei.png)

###### Abstract

Autonomous agents powered by foundation models can reason, plan, and use
tools, but their fixed weights and context limits hinder sustained
learning from experience over long-term deployment. While external
memory systems attempt to bridge this gap, they mostly act as passive
retrieval caches, evaluated primarily by whether they can recall stored
facts or past interactions. Whether memory can drive genuine continual
learning remains an open question. In this paper, we introduce
Memory-Centric Continual Learning (MCCL), arguing that memory should be
judged by whether it improves future actions, not merely what it
retrieves. MCCL has three main parts. First, a three-axis taxonomy
organizes memory by where state is stored (substrates), what form it
takes (assets), and how it is managed (policies). This defines a
two-loop lifecycle that updates memory state in an inner loop while
refining management policies in an outer loop based on task feedback.
Second, an evaluation protocol measures retention under interference,
adaptation to change, and task transfer to isolate genuine behavioral
gains from simple context expansion. Third, a memory scaling framework
models how agent performance scales with experience, storage capacity,
and compute budgets across non-parametric and parametric memory.
Together, these components reframe agent memory from passive lookup to
continuous learning, offering a principled route toward self-improving
autonomous agents.

## 1 The Landscape: Why Agents Need Continual Learning via Memory

Autonomous agents powered by foundation models have demonstrated strong
capabilities in multi-step reasoning, planning, and tool use across
digital and physical domains [Agashe et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib79), [Wang
et al. \[2024b\]](#bib.bib38), [Yao et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib3).
However, current agents cannot reliably improve from experience over
long deployments. Although modern agent harnesses can systematically
track execution logs, timestamps, and environment states, they often
treat accumulated history as raw text to be retrieved and inserted back
into the prompt. Simply retrieving raw text does not guarantee
behavioral improvement. As interaction trajectories grow, unmanaged
context dilutes relevant signals, increases inference latency, and
degrades model attention [Wang et al. \[2026d\]](#bib.bib43).

Conventional adaptation methods face clear trade-offs. Expanding context
windows is bounded by session limits and suffers from attention
degradation over long contexts. Meanwhile, continual fine-tuning
requires substantial computation, risks catastrophic forgetting of
general abilities, and lacks reliable mechanisms to roll back errors [Gu
et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib33), [Luo et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib32). Without
mechanisms to distill, update, and transfer accumulated experience,
deployed agents cannot retain what they learn. Consequently, they
repeatedly commit the same errors, struggle with conflicting or outdated
information, and fail to improve across sequential tasks [Cattan et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib30), [Hu et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib115), [Yu et al.
\[2026a\]](#bib.bib109).

Agent memory has emerged as a primary approach to support persistence
across sessions and personalization [Chhikara et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib17), [Packer et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib16), [Park et
al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib14). However, despite rapid progress, most
existing methods focus on specific engineering implementations rather
than systematic continual learning. Many approaches adopt an information
retrieval view, treating memory simply as an external database or a
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) cache designed to feed past tokens
back into the prompt [Lewis et al. \[2020\]](#bib.bib105), [Luo et al.
\[2026\]](#bib.bib128). Furthermore, existing classifications often blur
distinct system choices, including where memory is physically stored,
what form knowledge takes, and how memory operations are managed.
Evaluation also remains limited. Many benchmarks primarily test whether
an agent can recall or answer questions over a static past history,
rather than whether experience accumulated during deployment improves
subsequent actions under interference and drift [Du et al.
\[2024\]](#bib.bib99), [Maharana et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib97), [Wu et
al. \[2025a\]](#bib.bib98). Moreover, as memory stores grow, unmanaged
external memory suffers from retrieval noise, memory pollution, and
negative transfer when task distributions shift [Chen et al.
\[2026a\]](#bib.bib60), [Zhang et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib61).

To bridge this gap, we propose the framework of Memory-Centric Continual
Learning (MCCL). We argue that addressing the stability–plasticity
dilemma does not strictly require continuous gradient updates to
foundation model weights. Instead, memory itself can serve as an
evolving learning infrastructure through which an agent incorporates
feedback, refines strategies, and resolves knowledge conflicts during
deployment, while keeping the base model intact. In this framework,
continual learning operates as a coupled dynamical system. An inner
_memory-state loop_ updates stored knowledge from sequential deployment
experience, while an outer _policy-adaptation loop_ refines memory
management policies based on task performance. By connecting task
outcomes back to memory management, MCCL provides a principled route
toward Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI), where agents not only
accumulate experience but also improve how that experience is organized
and used. We summarize our core contributions as follows.

1.  1.  A unified taxonomy of memory architecture. We clarify existing
        classifications through a three-axis taxonomy: _where_ experience
        persists (substrates: contextual, activation, external addressable,
        and adaptive parametric), _what_ form it takes (assets: episodic,
        semantic, and procedural), and _how_ operations are managed
        (policies: prescribed, deliberative, and learned). This separates
        physical storage from cognitive representation and operational
        control (Section
        [2](#S2 "2 A Unified Framework: Architecting Memory for Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
2.  2.  The memory lifecycle as an engine for continual learning. We
        formalize memory dynamics across five core stages: selective
        formation, structural consolidation, reactivation and utilization,
        knowledge revision and selective forgetting, and feedback-driven
        policy evolution. This formulation explains how raw interactions
        turn into generalizable knowledge while mitigating memory pollution
        and drift (Section
        [3](#S3 "3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
3.  3.  A behavioral evaluation framework. We distinguish retrieval-level
        checks from genuine continual learning outcomes. We introduce a
        four-gate retention cascade (preservation, access, utilization, and
        effect) to diagnose failure points, use matched
        stateful-versus-reset comparisons ($`\Delta P_{t}`$) to isolate the
        effect of accumulated memory, and establish evaluation protocols to
        measure retention under interference, adaptation under drift, and
        cross-task transfer (Section
        [4](#S4 "4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
4.  4.  A memory scaling law. We model how agent performance scales with
        experience, storage capacity, and compute budgets across both
        non-parametric and parametric memory. Both share a resource-limited
        scaling frontier, but differ in how new writes interact with
        existing content. This shifts the focus from whether larger memory
        helps to how experience, capacity, and compute should scale together
        (Section
        [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).

In summary, this paper reframes agent memory from a passive storage
component into an active learning infrastructure, focusing on whether an
agent can continually learn over time rather than simply retrieve past
records. The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. Section
[2](#S2 "2 A Unified Framework: Architecting Memory for Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
presents the taxonomy across substrates, assets, and policies. Section
[3](#S3 "3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
details the memory lifecycle as a continual learning engine. Section
[4](#S4 "4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
formalizes the evaluation framework and memory scaling laws. Section
[5](#S5 "5 Memory-Centric Continual Learning Applications Across Digital and Physical Domains ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
analyzes applications across digital and physical domains. Section
[6](#S6 "6 Open Challenges in Memory-Centric Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
discusses open challenges in credit assignment, parametric synergy, and
trustworthy memory governance. Section
[7](#S7 "7 Conclusion ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
concludes the paper.

Figure 2: Memory-centric continual learning as a coupled dynamical
system. A task stream is executed by the agent and produces outcome
feedback. Experience is organized as episodic, semantic, and procedural
assets that persist across contextual, activation, external, and
parametric substrates. An inner memory-state loop forms, consolidates,
reactivates, and revises these assets, while prescribed, deliberative,
or learned policies govern the corresponding operations. An outer
policy-adaptation loop uses task feedback to update memory management.

## 2 A Unified Framework: Architecting Memory for Continual Learning

Classical continual learning theory has primarily focused on the
stability–plasticity dilemma within model weights, seeking to mitigate
catastrophic forgetting during sequential gradient updates [McCloskey
and Cohen \[1989\]](#bib.bib106), [Wang et al. \[2024c\]](#bib.bib108).
In contrast, foundation-model-based agents decouple computation from
persistent storage, distributing experiential adaptation across physical
media, semantic abstractions, and control mechanisms. However, existing
taxonomies often mix these levels, confusing where an experience is
stored with what form it takes and how it is managed [Huang et al.
\[2026\]](#bib.bib1), [Zhoubian et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib2). To
establish a principled foundation for memory-centric continual learning,
we organize the design space around three core axes, covering _where_
experience persists, _what_ reusable form it takes, and _how_ memory
operations are managed (Figure
[2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1 The Landscape: Why Agents Need Continual Learning via Memory ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).

- •
  Memory Substrates (WHERE): Physical Storage Media. Substrates define
  the physical medium where experience persists. They govern trade-offs
  between write latency, volatility, capacity, and durability,
  determining whether experience is held in active context, model
  activations, external stores, or model parameters.
- •
  Memory Assets (WHAT): Cognitive Representations. Assets describe the
  form knowledge takes. Independent of physical substrates, assets
  balance specific detail against general abstraction, grouping
  experience into episodic traces, semantic facts, or procedural skills.
- •
  Memory Policies (HOW): Mechanisms for Memory Management. Policies
  define the rules for writing, consolidating, retrieving, updating, and
  forgetting memories. They span fixed heuristics (prescribed), runtime
  language model reasoning (deliberative), and feedback-driven control
  (learned), determining whether memory management remains static or
  adapts over time.

These three axes are complementary dimensions rather than isolated
categories. A concrete agent memory system combines specific choices
across all three axes, such as compiling episodic traces into an
external addressable store governed by deliberative policies powered by
Large Language Models (LLMs). Decoupling these dimensions clarifies that
possessing an external substrate does not by itself yield transferable
capability, nor does storing an episodic asset indicate that an agent
has an effective policy to retrieve and use it. By formalizing this
three-axis architecture, we establish a principled framework to diagnose
where existing architectures succeed or fail under non-stationary
streams, and to compose hybrid systems that support RSI.

### 2.1 Memory Substrates: Physical Foundations for Stability and Plasticity

Memory substrates define where experience persists and how it can be
accessed and modified. Existing taxonomies commonly separate internal
from external memory, or computation-coupled from addressable memory
[Huang et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib1), [Zhoubian et al.
\[2026\]](#bib.bib2). We distinguish substrates by where state
physically persists, rather than how it appears during inference. For
example, text retrieved from a database remains external memory even
when placed in a prompt; conversely, offloading the KV cache to disk
changes its storage location but keeps it an activation-level
representation. Following this principle, we identify four basic
substrates: contextual memory, activation memory, external addressable
memory, and adaptive parametric memory.

This distinction is independent of memory assets and policies. A
substrate specifies how long experience can persist, how it can be
addressed, and at what granularity it can be updated. It does not, by
itself, tell us whether those updates yield transferable improvement;
that depends on the assets built from experience and on the policies
that govern their formation and use.

##### Contextual memory.

The most direct memory substrate is the sequence of tokens currently
visible to the model. Thoughts, actions, and observations can be
recorded in a shared prompt so that the trajectory influences subsequent
decisions through self-attention [Yao et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib3).
Ever-growing traces are replaced with subgoal-level summaries or
compressed reasoning states [Hu et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib4), [Wu et al.
\[2025b\]](#bib.bib5), and deletion, compression, and rewriting are
exposed as agent actions [Zhang et al. \[2025b\]](#bib.bib6). These
methods require little architectural modification and offer nearly
immediate plasticity, with relatively high fidelity over short horizons.
Their stability, however, is bounded by the session limit. As
trajectories grow, older observations consume context tokens and degrade
model attention. Even within the supported context window, performance
can drop sharply with sequence length, evidence position, and task
complexity [Liu et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib25), [Hsieh et al.
\[2024\]](#bib.bib26). Summarization and compaction extend the effective
horizon but introduce an irreversible, often lossy selection step [Cim
et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib27).

##### Activation memory.

A second family compresses history into machine-native states, including
hidden activations, KV caches, recurrent states, and dedicated memory
slots. Hidden states are reused across segments, and KV entries are
selectively retained under a fixed budget [Dai et al.
\[2019\]](#bib.bib7), [Zhang et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib8), [Liu et al.
\[2025c\]](#bib.bib9). Previously computed internal representations are
placed in a retrievable memory bank [Wang et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib10),
[Wu et al. \[2022\]](#bib.bib11), and latent pools or designated slots
are introduced that can be repeatedly read and updated [Wang et al.
\[2024e\]](#bib.bib12), [Kang et al. \[2025b\]](#bib.bib13). Compared
with textual context, these states participate in the forward pass at
higher bandwidth and need not be translated back into natural language.
Gating, state transitions, and isolated slots can also confine rapid
adaptation to a restricted state channel, leaving the pretrained
backbone unchanged. This efficiency shifts the capacity cost to runtime
memory and limits controllability: activation memories are tied to a
particular model, layer structure, and representation space, and their
contents are difficult to inspect, edit at the level of individual
facts, or transfer across models [Kwon et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib28).
Fixed-dimensional latent states also face capacity limits as experience
accumulates. Offloading activation states or the KV cache to disk does
not turn them into external text memory, because they can still only be
processed as internal representations by the original model.

##### External addressable memory.

External memory separates storage from model computation. Experience
persists as independently maintained entries that can be explicitly
retrieved and modified. Implementations range from natural-language logs
and summaries to vector indices, relational tables, knowledge graphs,
hierarchical trees, code repositories, and collections of specialized
stores. Observation streams are combined with higher-order reflections,
language-level diagnoses are retained from failed trials, and
information is moved between a bounded active context and larger
archival stores [Park et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib14), [Shinn et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib15), [Packer et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib16). Memory
extraction, dynamic organization, and structured retrieval are further
developed in recent systems [Chhikara et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib17), [Xu
et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib18), [Gutiérrez et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib19),
[Rezazadeh et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib20). This substrate offers a direct
route to non-parametric continual learning: new experience can survive
across sessions without altering the foundation model, individual
records can be revised or versioned, and capacity can grow independently
of model size.

Structural separation changes rather than resolves the
stability–plasticity dilemma. External stores avoid catastrophic
forgetting caused by parameter updates, but an old memory can become
inaccessible when its retrieval rank declines, producing a form of
_functional forgetting_. Summarization, deduplication, and conflict
resolution may also overwrite earlier records or change whether they
still count as valid evidence. As the store grows, redundant, stale, and
conflicting entries raise retrieval noise. Indexing, provenance
tracking, and deletion become more expensive as well. Irrelevant or
conflicting retrieval can be actively harmful [Wang et al.
\[2024a\]](#bib.bib29), [Cattan et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib30), and
current memory agents do not reliably combine retrieval, online
updating, long-range use, and selective forgetting [Hu et al.
\[2026b\]](#bib.bib102), [Uddin et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib31).

##### Adaptive parametric memory.

Parametric substrates encode deployment experience in model weights,
detachable adapters, or designated fast parameters. Static pretrained
weights are not sufficient for this category; the defining property is
that parameters can still be updated from experience after deployment.
Consecutive edits are isolated in a side parameter space, and behavior
obtained with visible context is distilled into a model that no longer
receives that context [Wang et al. \[2024d\]](#bib.bib21), [Wang et al.
\[2024f\]](#bib.bib22). Test-time neural memory and rapidly updated LoRA
parameters are used to preserve behavioral effects after the
corresponding evidence has left the active prompt [Behrouz et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib23), [Ren et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib24). Once
internalized, it can be used during a standard forward pass without
external retrieval and may generalize beyond the specific instance that
produced the update. It is therefore attractive for stable, frequently
reused, and well-validated knowledge or skills.

This efficiency at read time comes at the cost of more expensive and
less controllable writes. Parameter updates require optimization,
generated supervision, or specialized test-time learning rules. Once an
experience is distributed across weights, its scope is difficult to
localize, and record-level expiration, provenance tracking, and deletion
become substantially harder than in an external store. Repeated
adaptation can cause catastrophic forgetting of earlier knowledge or
consolidate transient feedback and self-generated errors into persistent
behavior. Continual instruction tuning forgets across domain knowledge,
reasoning, and reading comprehension, and successful factual edits can
still degrade unrelated general abilities [Luo et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib32), [Gu et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib33). Side memories,
low-rank adapters, and slow–fast parameter decompositions reduce
forgetting by restricting the update scope, but introduce routing
failures, module-merging conflicts, and long-term drift of their own.

##### Hybrid substrates.

Practical memory systems increasingly combine substrates with
complementary roles, where tokens and activations support immediate
adaptation, external stores provide durable and auditable retention, and
parameter updates selectively internalize stable skills [Packer et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib16), [Wang et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib10), [Behrouz et
al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib23), [Ren et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib24). Existing
hybrid systems still leave several design choices open, including where
new experience first enters, when it moves to a more durable store, how
queries are routed, which state is authoritative during conflicts, and
where erroneous updates can be rolled back.

Figure 3: A unified taxonomy of agent memory for continual learning.
Memory substrates specify where experience persists, memory assets
describe what reusable representations are constructed from experience,
and memory policies determine how memory is written, retrieved,
consolidated, revised, and forgotten. Representative related works are
organized by subclass under each of the three dimensions.

### 2.2 Memory Assets: Representations for Cross-Task Transfer

Memory substrates determine the physical state in which experience
persists, whereas memory assets describe the reusable units constructed
from that experience. The two dimensions do not admit a fixed
correspondence. The same task experience may remain temporarily as
context tokens, be written to an external log, organized as a graph, or
eventually internalized into model parameters. Conversely, a single
external store may contain event records, factual relations, and
executable skills. We therefore classify an asset by how it is used when
retrieved, rather than by how it is stored, distinguishing episodic,
semantic, and procedural assets. This taxonomy concerns experience that
can persist across interactions or tasks. Contextual tokens and
activation states may participate in memory formation, but they count as
assets only once they persist in a reusable form across tasks; otherwise
they remain working state on a substrate.

As with substrates, possessing an asset is not itself evidence of
cross-task learning. Episodes, facts, and skills specify the _form_ in
which experience can transfer; whether transfer occurs is a question for
memory policies.

##### Episodic assets.

Episodic assets preserve _what happened_. Their basic unit is a
particular experience situated in its temporal, environmental, and task
context. An episode may include observations, goals, actions, tool
calls, intermediate reasoning, feedback, and outcomes, or a compressed
segment derived from these elements. Compared with more abstract assets,
episodes retain the conditions under which an experience was obtained.
They therefore support case-based reuse, resumption of interrupted
tasks, and retrospective inspection of the evidence behind a later
belief or strategy. Observations and higher-order reflections are stored
in a memory stream, trial outcomes and linguistic diagnoses are retained
after failed attempts, and both prior trajectories and insights
extracted from collections of experience are retrieved for later reuse
[Park et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib14), [Shinn et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib15),
[Zhao et al. \[2024a\]](#bib.bib34). Structured event records and
production memory stores further retain temporally grounded episodes
across sessions [Lu et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib35), [Chhikara et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib17).

Episodes are particularly valuable for rare events and one-shot
observations for which reliable abstraction is not yet possible. They
permit highly plastic writes because the agent need not decide
immediately whether an observation expresses a general rule, while their
high fidelity supplies evidence for later correction. The same fidelity,
however, produces substantial storage and retrieval costs. As the
collection grows, trajectories that are superficially similar but
decisionally different compete for retrieval, while complete traces
carry observations and actions irrelevant to the current problem. Stored
experience is most useful when its structure matches the target task; in
other settings, long-context baselines remain competitive [Wang et al.
\[2026d\]](#bib.bib43).

##### Semantic assets.

Semantic assets preserve _what is known_. Their reusable units are
facts, concepts, relations, constraints, preferences, and environment
models abstracted from particular observations. Unlike episodic assets,
they suppress much of the temporal and surface detail of the originating
event so that knowledge can be reused across sessions and tasks. A
recurring user preference, a tool authentication requirement, or a
relation between two entities can thus be invoked without replaying the
full interaction from which it was inferred.

Stripping temporal and situational detail yields compression and broader
reuse, and structured semantic assets additionally support entity-level
revision, relational inference, and multi-hop retrieval. Dynamically
indexed notes are linked so that new entries can modify the contextual
representation of existing ones; current and historical relations are
maintained in temporal knowledge graphs; and structured retrieval is
framed as non-parametric continual learning [Xu et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib18), [Rasmussen et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib36),
[Gutiérrez et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib19). Hierarchical schemas and
operating-system-style stores likewise organize reusable facts beyond a
single episode [Rezazadeh et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib20), [Kang et al.
\[2025a\]](#bib.bib126). These approaches indicate that semantic memory
is not a static knowledge base but a knowledge state that must be
continually formed, connected, and revised.

The central weakness of semantic assets arises from the same abstraction
that enables transfer. Premature consolidation may convert an accidental
correlation into a general claim, while removing context may discard
when, where, and with what confidence that claim is valid. Appending new
information can leave mutually competing facts in memory, whereas
overwriting an earlier entry can destroy historical provenance.
Retrieving updated evidence also does not guarantee recognition of an
implicitly invalidated belief or corresponding adaptation in downstream
behavior [Chao et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib37). In practice, existing
semantic stores often keep the extracted claim while omitting
provenance, temporal scope, confidence, and links to the episodes that
support it.

##### Procedural assets.

Procedural assets preserve _how to act_. Their reusable units include
workflows, strategies, plan templates, tool-use rules, and executable
programs. They may be represented as natural-language instructions,
code, parameterized workflows, or behavioral policies internalized in
model parameters. The distinction from semantic assets is functional
rather than syntactic: semantic assets describe a state of the world,
whereas procedural assets prescribe how that state should be changed
under particular conditions. The same piece of text can therefore be
semantic if retrieved as a fact and procedural if retrieved as an
instruction.

Because they directly constrain action generation, procedural assets
provide the most immediate form of behavioral transfer.
Environment-verified skills are stored as composable code, parameterized
workflows are induced from successful trajectories for reuse across
tasks and domains, and generalizable strategies are extracted from
successful and failed experience instead of retaining only raw
trajectories [Wang et al. \[2024b\]](#bib.bib38), [Wang et al.
\[2025c\]](#bib.bib39), [Ouyang et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib40). Evolving
context playbooks and learned memory skills further compile repeated
execution into reusable action knowledge [Zhang et al.
\[2026f\]](#bib.bib46), [Zhang et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib127), [Ni et
al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib80). Procedural assets can also be composed,
allowing previously acquired skills to become components of more complex
behavior and reducing repeated exploration.

This compilation nevertheless locks in assumptions from the original
experience. A workflow may remain valid only for a particular tool
version, interface, or set of environment dynamics. Retrieving a skill
for a superficially similar task with different preconditions can
produce negative transfer, while unverified self-reflection may turn an
accidental success into a persistent rule. Explicit skill maintenance is
not uniformly superior to retaining prior context, and gains are more
reliable on tasks with clear workflows than on open-ended ones [Guan et
al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib41), [Zhong et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib42). In
current systems, preconditions, expected outcomes, environment
dependencies, verification status, and versioning are often left
implicit. Long-lived skill libraries therefore tend to accumulate skills
rather than merge, deprecate, or regression-test them.

##### Cross-asset consolidation.

The three asset classes are not mutually exclusive storage compartments.
They describe experience at different levels of abstraction. Episodic
assets retain concrete evidence; regularities recurring across episodes
may be consolidated into semantic assets; and repeatedly validated
action regularities may be compiled into procedural assets. This
transformation improves compression and cross-task reuse while
progressively removing details of the original context. Asset formation
therefore exposes a fundamental trade-off: representations close to
individual episodes preserve evidence but depend on precise matching,
whereas abstract knowledge and skills are cheaper to invoke but allow
erroneous generalizations to affect a wider range of tasks.

Consolidation is rarely a clean, one-way pipeline in existing systems.
Semantic claims and procedural skills often lose their links to
supporting episodes, and new experience cannot always qualify, revise,
or revoke what was previously consolidated. A single record can also
contain all three components, such as a task trajectory, an
environmental fact inferred from it, and a corrective procedure learned
after failure. At retrieval time, such a record is better classified by
how it is used than by how it is physically stored.

### 2.3 Memory Policies: Prescribed, Deliberative, and Learned Control

As agents operate over longer horizons, memory is no longer only a
problem of storing more information or retrieving it more accurately.
The central challenge is how memory should change over time, deciding
what should be retained, consolidated, revised, or forgotten, and how
these decisions should adapt to new experience and feedback. We refer to
the mechanisms governing such decisions as memory policies. They
determine when and how memory state evolves, and they correspond to the
_how_ column of Figure
[3](#S2.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ Hybrid substrates. ‣ 2.1 Memory Substrates: Physical Foundations for Stability and Plasticity ‣ 2 A Unified Framework: Architecting Memory for Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents").

We distinguish three control regimes. _Prescribed_ policies apply fixed
rules, heuristics, and scoring functions. _Deliberative_ policies use
the LLM to decide, for each case, whether to write, retrieve, or revise,
given the task, memory state, and context. _Learned_ policies update
memory control from outcomes and feedback. The regimes are compositional
rather than exclusive: a system may prescribe capacity limits,
deliberate over conflicts, and learn retrieval utilities. They also
align with the prescribed, deliberative, and learned control tiers used
later in the lifecycle comparison (Section
[3](#S3 "3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).

##### Prescribed policies.

Prescribed policies treat memory management as resource allocation under
limited context, cache, and store capacity. Control is specified before
deployment through rules such as FIFO, LRU, sliding windows, or fixed
Top-$`k`$ retrieval, so utility is approximated by position, recency,
and budget rather than estimated future value [Xiao et al.
\[2024\]](#bib.bib44). Salience-based scoring is a richer but still
prescribed variant: recency, importance, and relevance are combined to
select episodic records [Park et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib14), [Lewis et
al. \[2020\]](#bib.bib105), and entries are admitted or forgotten
according to novelty, importance, or decay thresholds [Zhong et al.
\[2024\]](#bib.bib100), [Tan et al. \[2025b\]](#bib.bib53). Hierarchical
designs go beyond single-shot Top-$`k`$ by controlling movement across
memory tiers, yet the tools, thresholds, and control rules remain
predefined [Packer et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib16). Prescribed control is
efficient, predictable, and easier to inspect. Its limitation is
rigidity: what counts as novel, important, or disposable is not
subsequently revised according to task outcomes.

##### Deliberative policies.

Deliberative policies place the LLM inside the memory-control loop
because numerical scores cannot fully capture the semantic value of an
experience. The model is asked, for each case, whether to store,
retrieve, resolve a conflict, or rewrite, using the current task, memory
state, and context. Handcrafted scoring is replaced by case-by-case
judgments, while the outer tool protocol remains fixed [Packer et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib16), [Xu et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib18), [Chhikara et
al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib17). A stronger deliberative pattern lets
experience revise subsequent control in natural language: verbal
post-mortems are stored to steer later attempts, and successful and
failed traces are abstracted into reusable strategies [Shinn et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib15), [Ouyang et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib40). Write
boundaries are tightened, consistency is verified against constraint
memory, or the memory collection itself is reorganized [Liu et al.
\[2025a\]](#bib.bib119), [Fan et al. \[2025a\]](#bib.bib120), [Nan et
al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib121), [Kang et al. \[2025c\]](#bib.bib45). The
resulting control is flexible and interpretable, but inference-intensive
and dependent on prompt following. Rewriting a strategy in language
still leaves the controller untrained, as most such systems lack credit
assignment from later task outcomes back to earlier memory decisions.

##### Learned policies.

Learned policies treat memory control as an experience-updated decision
problem. The agent may still use standard operations, such as write,
retrieve, compress, revise, and forget, but a trainable controller
selects among them, and those selections improve from outcomes and
feedback. Discrete memory actions are learned from reward [Yan et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib51), [Zhang et al. \[2025b\]](#bib.bib6), [Yu et al.
\[2026b\]](#bib.bib56), and purely similarity-based retrieval is
replaced with utility values that reflect historical contribution to
success [Zhang et al. \[2026g\]](#bib.bib125). Reinforcement learning
provides the credit assignment that static controllers lack, because the
true value of a write or deletion often appears only several steps later
[Yao et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib123), [Yuan et al. \[2025a\]](#bib.bib52),
[Chen et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib124).

Two levels of learning should be distinguished. In many existing systems
the controller is optimized offline and then frozen, so memory content
continues to evolve while the operational policy remains static. Recent
RSI frameworks instead attempt rule-level modification, where the
language model is prompted to inspect and revise its own prompt
templates or execution scaffolding [Zhang et al. \[2026f\]](#bib.bib46),
[Agrawal et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib49), [Yin et al.
\[2024\]](#bib.bib47), [Zhang et al. \[2026d\]](#bib.bib48). Recuris
further shows that a Meta-Agent can synthesize validation-gated updates
from execution failures, recursively refining operational rules and the
skill library [Yu et al. \[2026c\]](#bib.bib50). Although
reflection-based prompt adaptation offers substantial semantic
flexibility, such methods typically rely on heuristic textual edits and
trial-and-error screening against a validation set. Under long-horizon
delayed task feedback, they struggle to establish fine-grained temporal
credit assignment and to keep the meta-policy stable. A more systematic
form of continual policy evolution would update the memory-management
policy from ongoing environmental feedback while suppressing policy
drift. The next section turns to how experience-level rule evolution can
be combined with structured closed-loop policy learning across the
lifecycle.

## 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning

The previous section describes agent memory in terms of where experience
persists, what reusable form it takes, and how memory operations are
controlled. These dimensions define the architecture of a memory system,
but they do not explain how that system changes through continued
deployment. Continual learning is often described as adaptation through
parameter updates. For agents, however, experience can also accumulate
in external records, persistent internal states, and other writable
memory substrates. We therefore view memory-centric continual learning
(MCCL) as a coupled dynamical process, where experience updates the
persistent memory state while behavioral feedback can also adapt the
policy governing future memory decisions.

At the first level, newly acquired experience changes the memory state
through a transition function controlled by the current memory policy,

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\mathcal{M}_{t+1}=\mathcal{T}\left(\mathcal{M}_{t},e_{t};\pi_{t}^{\mathrm{mem}}\right),
``` |  | (3.1) |

where $`\mathcal{M}_{t}`$ denotes the persistent memory state at time
$`t`$, $`e_{t}`$ denotes experience from the current interaction, and
$`\pi_{t}^{\mathrm{mem}}`$ denotes the policy that controls how this
experience is handled. We use $`e_{t}`$ broadly to include relevant
observations, agent actions, outcomes, and feedback. The transition
function $`\mathcal{T}`$ is independent of any particular memory
substrate. Depending on the system, it may admit new experience,
reorganize accumulated memory, revise existing content, or reduce the
future influence of selected memories.

A change in memory state is not, by itself, evidence of continual
learning. The updated state must later be accessed and incorporated into
reasoning, decisions, or actions under the memory policy,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{M}_{t+1}\xrightarrow{\text{access and utilization }(\pi_{t+k}^{\mathrm{mem}})}\Delta\mathcal{B}_{t+k},\qquad k>0,
``` |  |

where $`\Delta\mathcal{B}_{t+k}`$ denotes a downstream behavioral effect
associated with the memory change. Information may persist without being
retrieved, may be retrieved without being used appropriately, or may
influence behavior in a harmful way. Memory supports continual learning
only when experience-induced changes remain usable, yield appropriate
improvement or adaptation in later behavior, and avoid unnecessary
damage to still-useful capabilities [Shu et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib110),
[Yu et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib109).

At the second level, feedback may change not only memory content but
also the policy that controls future memory decisions. We write this
outer update as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\pi_{t+1}^{\mathrm{mem}}=\mathcal{U}\left(\pi_{t}^{\mathrm{mem}},\mathcal{F}_{t:t+H}\right),
``` |  | (3.2) |

where $`\mathcal{F}_{t:t+H}`$ summarizes downstream feedback observed
over a later horizon $`H`$, and $`\mathcal{U}`$ denotes the rule used to
update the memory policy. Such feedback may include task outcomes,
rewards, errors, user corrections, or other signals that reveal the
consequences of earlier memory decisions. $`\mathcal{U}`$ need not
change the policy in every system. A fixed controller may continue to
modify $`\mathcal{M}_{t}`$ while $`\pi_{t}^{\mathrm{mem}}`$ remains
unchanged. Policy evolution refers to the stronger case in which
accumulated experience also changes how future memory decisions are
made.

Equations
([3.1](#S3.E1 "Equation 3.1 ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))
and
([3.2](#S3.E2 "Equation 3.2 ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")),
together with behavioral utilization, define two coupled processes. The
inner memory-state loop governs both how the persistent state
$`\mathcal{M}_{t}`$ evolves and how it informs decisions: how experience
enters persistent memory (*formation and writing*), how accumulated
experience is restructured (*organization and consolidation*), how prior
memory returns to the active decision process (*retrieval and
utilization*), and how the stored state is maintained as new evidence
arrives (*revision and forgetting*). Persistent memory is not an
additional operational stage; rather, it is the central evolving state
upon which write, consolidation, readout, and maintenance operations
act. The outer control loop determines how decisions within these four
stages are governed and whether downstream feedback modifies those
control rules over time.

Together, the two loops describe continual learning as more than memory
accumulation. Experience changes memory, memory influences later
behavior, and behavioral consequences may in turn improve how subsequent
experience is managed. The goal is therefore not to maximize the amount
of stored information or the frequency of updates, but to support
adaptation while preserving valid evidence and useful capabilities.
Figure
[4](#S3.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
summarizes this view.

![Refer to caption](2610.29357v1/memory_survey_lifecycle.png)

Figure 4: The memory lifecycle as two coupled processes in
memory-centric continual learning. The memory-state loop governs how
experience enters persistent memory, is organized and reused, and is
revised or forgotten as conditions change. The control loop uses
behavioral outcomes to influence future memory decisions.

### 3.1 Formation and Selective Writing

Formation is the first point at which ongoing experience can acquire
persistent influence over future behavior. It determines not only which
observations should enter long-term memory, but also how they are
represented and what source, temporal, and contextual information is
preserved for later use.

##### Selective Writing and Plasticity Control.

Memory-centric continual learning begins with an admission decision,
because not every interaction should modify persistent memory. Writing
too freely can accumulate incidental, redundant, or erroneous
observations and increase interference over time. Writing too
conservatively can discard experience that would have supported later
adaptation. Selective writing therefore defines the agent’s plasticity
boundary by deciding which deployment-time experiences may exert
persistent influence on future behavior. Selected session-level
information can be promoted into persistent memory, and storage
operations can be exposed as actions that are optimized from downstream
outcomes [Cognee \[2026\]](#bib.bib55), [Yu et al.
\[2026b\]](#bib.bib56). These examples show that admission can range
from staged promotion to learned action selection. In either case, the
cost of a poor write can persist, as admitting unreliable experience
propagates errors while rejecting useful experience removes evidence
needed for future learning. The objective is therefore not to store as
much as possible, but to admit useful experience without giving
transient or unreliable observations unnecessary long-term influence.

##### Initial Representation.

Admission also requires a choice about how experience is represented.
What is made explicit at write time determines what can later be
retrieved, compared, or revised. An experience may remain as a
relatively direct episodic record or be encoded into a representation
that makes events, entities, relations, and temporal information
explicit [Lu et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib35), [Rasmussen et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib36). Structure and granularity are orthogonal choices:
structure determines how explicitly relational and temporal attributes
are indexed, whereas granularity determines how much situational detail
is retained versus compressed. A representation can therefore be highly
structured while remaining fine-grained, whereas overly coarse or
unstructured encodings may discard contextual details that later become
critical. Because subsequent consolidation cannot reliably recover
evidence that was never recorded at admission, the initial
representation directly bounds the evidence available to all downstream
memory operations.

##### Source, Scope, and Memory Pollution.

A useful persistent memory should also retain the conditions under which
it was acquired. Relevant information includes provenance, temporal
validity, contextual scope, and, where available, confidence [Li et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib57), [Rasmussen et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib36). These
attributes do not prove that a memory is true. Instead, they help later
reasoning determine where the memory came from, whether it still
applies, and how strongly it should be trusted. Without such
information, a hallucinated observation may later be reused as fact, an
accidental success may become a general strategy, or a temporary state
may be applied outside the context in which it was observed. This causes
memory pollution, where an error introduced at formation continues to
degrade later behavior and may be reinforced through repeated retrieval,
revision, and consolidation. Formation therefore determines both which
experience enters persistent memory and what evidence is carried forward
with it. The next question is how these formed memories should be
organized and transformed as experience accumulates.

### 3.2 Organization, Compression, and Consolidation

Organization and consolidation form the integration stage of the memory
lifecycle. Once experience has entered persistent memory, the system
must decide which observations belong together, when accumulated
evidence is ready for further transformation, and whether repeated
experience should remain episodic or become more reusable knowledge.
These decisions improve stability and reuse, but they also introduce
risks, as premature or overly aggressive consolidation can turn
incomplete evidence into persistent generalizations. Here, consolidation
refers to the durable organization or transformation of experience
across interactions, rather than the compression of active short-term
context alone.

##### Choosing the Unit of Experience.

Consolidation first requires a decision about the unit over which
experience is organized. Continuous interaction arrives as a stream, yet
memory systems must decide which observations should be treated as part
of the same event, episode, or semantic segment. This choice determines
which pieces of evidence can later be compared, linked, or transformed
together. Recent systems use dynamically detected events or temporally
grounded episodes instead of relying only on fixed turns or windows [Zou
et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib62), [Rasmussen et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib36).
Fine-grained units preserve local detail but can split dependencies
across several memories. Broader units make longer-range relations
easier to capture, but they may also combine observations that should
remain separate. Experience boundaries therefore shape what later
consolidation can learn from, determining which evidence is considered
jointly and which patterns can eventually be stabilized and reused.

##### When to Consolidate.

A coherent experience unit does not automatically justify immediate
consolidation. If transformation occurs too early, the system may
generalize from insufficient evidence, repeatedly rewrite unstable
knowledge, or amplify an early mistake. If it occurs too late, related
experience remains fragmented and becomes harder to reuse. Recent
approaches make this timing decision explicit in different ways.
LycheeMemory V2 waits for semantic segment boundaries before encoding
accumulated interaction into long-term memory, whereas RecMem uses
recurrence across related observations as a signal that consolidation is
warranted [Li et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib58), [Dai et al.
\[2026\]](#bib.bib59). These methods treat consolidation timing as an
explicit memory-control decision rather than a routine background
process. Their triggers, however, are still prescribed rather than
learned from downstream outcomes. This distinction separates the
question of when evidence is ready for consolidation from the later
policy-level question of whether the rule that makes this decision can
itself improve from feedback.

##### From Episodes to Reusable Abstractions.

Consolidation also determines the level at which past experience remains
available for future use. Individual episodes preserve what happened and
the conditions under which it happened, but continual learning often
requires regularities that can be reused beyond a single case.
Collections of trajectories can be distilled into reusable
natural-language insights or executable skills [Zhao et al.
\[2024a\]](#bib.bib34), [Wang et al. \[2024b\]](#bib.bib38). Evidence
from continual experience internalization, although obtained in a
parametric setting, similarly suggests that principle-level experience
may remain more robust across repeated updates than instance-specific
traces [Chen et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib60). Abstraction can therefore
improve transfer, but it should not be treated as a mandatory
replacement for episodic memory. Episodes preserve context and
supporting evidence, whereas more abstract knowledge trades some of that
detail for broader applicability. Consolidation is thus a change in the
form and scope of knowledge, not merely a reduction in storage size. It
determines when an experience should remain a case to be recalled and
when repeated evidence is strong enough to support a more general
principle, schema, or procedure.

##### Lossy Consolidation and Evidence Preservation.

The same transformation that improves reuse can also remove information
that later becomes important. Repeated LLM-based rewriting has been
shown to initially improve and then reduce memory utility, while
retaining raw episodes can remain competitive with systems that
repeatedly force consolidation [Zhang et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib61).
Compression can also remove the contextual conditions needed to decide
whether an abstracted memory still applies. RaMem describes this failure
as context collapse and restores information such as event time,
participants, and session scope before compressed memories are used as
evidence [Yang et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib63). Consolidation should
therefore preserve links to supporting evidence rather than replace that
evidence by default. Higher-level abstractions should remain traceable
to the episodes from which they were derived so that later retrieval,
verification, or revision can recover from a poor generalization.
Consolidation can stabilize and generalize experience, but it can also
become a source of distortion, interference, and implicit forgetting
when abstraction removes information that remains useful.

### 3.3 Retrieval and Utilization

Retrieval and utilization (i.e., uptake) connect accumulated experience
to the agent’s current reasoning and actions. Their effectiveness
depends on recovering sufficient evidence, matching it to the current
decision, and using it appropriately. These functions concern the use of
existing memory rather than changes to its persistent content.

##### Reactivating Relevant Evidence.

Retrieval must recover usable evidence, not simply records with similar
wording. A task may depend on several related events, their temporal
order, or the circumstances in which earlier observations were made.
Selecting the right memory source is therefore only part of the problem.
The system must also recover enough supporting context to interpret what
it finds. Temporal graph memories, reflective memory managers, and
contextual-reinstatement methods offer different ways to select or
recover such evidence [Rasmussen et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib36), [Tan et
al. \[2025b\]](#bib.bib53), [Yang et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib63). These
mechanisms connect retrieval to the representation and
evidence-preservation choices discussed above. A compact summary may be
sufficient for one task, whereas another may require access to its
supporting episodes. Recovering that context helps establish the meaning
and scope of the memory without rewriting it. The goal is not to return
as much history as possible, but to recover enough relevant evidence and
to keep its source and context checkable.

##### Matching Memory to the Decision State.

A memory can be relevant to a task yet unhelpful at a particular
decision point. During planning, the agent may need a reusable procedure
or a record of earlier constraints. During tool execution, it may
instead need exact parameters or the conditions required for an
operation to succeed. Verification may call for evidence that tests a
tentative conclusion, while failure recovery may benefit from records of
unsuccessful attempts. Retrieval should therefore consider the current
subgoal, the evidence already available, and the uncertainty that
prevents the next decision. This also dictates when retrieval is
beneficial, since additional memory may resolve a specific information
gap, repeat existing context, or introduce distracting details.
Relevance to the overall task is therefore not enough. The retrieved
evidence should address what the agent needs to decide at that moment,
rather than simply resemble its current query.

##### From Access to Effective Use.

Utilization (often termed *uptake* in retrieval workflows) concerns
whether and how retrieved evidence actually shapes reasoning or action.
A record appearing in the context does not establish that the agent used
it, and its use does not guarantee behavioral improvement. Even after a
relevant record is preserved and retrieved, the agent may overlook it in
context, misinterpret its valid scope, or misapply a past strategy under
conditions where it no longer holds. Studies of continual agents and
experience reuse highlight this gap between retaining experience and
benefiting from it later [Shu et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib110), [Chen et
al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib60). Consequently, four sequential conditions
must remain conceptually distinct: *preservation* (retaining the record
in storage), *access* (retrieving it into the decision context),
*utilization* (incorporating it into reasoning), and *behavioral effect*
(achieving a net task improvement, formalized as the retention cascade
in Section
[4](#S4 "4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
Appropriate utilization may involve applying a memory, restricting its
use to supported conditions, or rejecting it when more reliable evidence
contradicts it. When memory use reveals an error or outdated assumption
that would also harm future tasks, the system must decide whether and
how to modify persistent memory. That maintenance decision is the
subject of revision and forgetting.

### 3.4 Revision and Forgetting

Revision and forgetting maintain persistent memory as new evidence
arrives and earlier knowledge loses its validity or usefulness. Revision
changes what is recorded or treated as current, while forgetting
regulates continued access and influence. These operations contribute to
the state transition in Equation
([3.1](#S3.E1 "Equation 3.1 ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
They may be triggered by incoming experience or by problems discovered
during memory use.

##### Resolving Conflicts and Tracking Current Validity.

Before modifying memory, the system must determine what the new evidence
changes. A disagreement may indicate an error, a change over time, or a
difference in context. These cases require different responses.
Correcting a false observation is not the same as preserving a
historically accurate observation while marking it as no longer current.
Graphiti separates temporal validity from the retention of historical
evidence, while UMA processes successive observations to maintain an
evolving memory state [Rasmussen et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib36), [Zhang et
al. \[2026e\]](#bib.bib64). Source reliability and applicability must
also be considered; newer information should not automatically replace
older evidence. When the available evidence does not resolve a conflict,
preserving the competing records with their sources may be preferable to
forcing an update. The goal is to maintain usable current knowledge
without erasing the conditions under which earlier memories were valid.

##### Making Knowledge Revision Persistent.

Once a correction is justified, it should be reflected in persistent
memory so that later tasks do not repeatedly encounter the same
inconsistency. Revision can add missing information, correct an existing
record, narrow its scope, change its validity, or reorganize its links
to other memories. Systems implement these changes through direct
updates or through mechanisms that retain earlier evidence and versions
[Zhang et al. \[2026e\]](#bib.bib64), [Sun et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib65),
[Li et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib57). These options need not be mutually
exclusive. A system can update its current knowledge while retaining the
history needed to inspect or reverse the change. Revision should also
account for derived memories. If an episode supports a summary or
reusable procedure, correcting the episode alone may leave that derived
knowledge inconsistent. Links to supporting evidence help identify which
derived memories may also need review. The objective is not frequent
rewriting, but a justified change whose scope matches the evidence and
whose consequences remain traceable.

##### Expiration, Suppression, and Deletion.

Not every maintenance decision concerns factual truth. A memory can
remain accurate yet become redundant, rarely useful, or overly costly to
keep active. Forgetting regulates whether and how such records continue
to influence later behavior. Hard deletion permanently removes a record
from its persistent store; expiration deactivates it according to a
predefined retention window or inactivity schedule; and soft suppression
retains the record while lowering its retrieval priority or
accessibility. MemoryBank uses time-dependent memory strength decay,
whereas VerMem supports soft deletion with retained history [Zhong et
al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib100), [Sun et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib65). These
capacity- and utility-driven forgetting mechanisms differ fundamentally
from epistemic *temporal invalidation* during revision. Invalidation
updates the temporal or contextual scope of a claim to record that it no
longer holds in the current state, without erasing the historical
evidence or evicting the record. Although both operations can reduce a
memory’s future influence, factual validity (whether a claim still
holds) and retrieval availability (whether a record remains accessible)
are distinct properties. Choosing among these operations requires
diagnosing whether the issue is outdated knowledge, unwanted retrieval
interference, or storage cost.

##### Selective Forgetting and Capability Preservation.

Intentional forgetting aims to reduce unwanted influence, whereas
catastrophic forgetting refers to the unintended loss of knowledge or
capabilities that remain useful after further learning [Luo et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib32). In external memory, useful information may also
become functionally unavailable even when the record survives [Yu et al.
\[2026a\]](#bib.bib109). The presence of a Delete action in systems such
as AgeMem and UMA establishes a possible operation, not an effective
policy for selective forgetting [Yu et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib56),
[Zhang et al. \[2026e\]](#bib.bib64). Nor does deleting a source record
necessarily remove its influence from summaries or procedures derived
from it. Conversely, consolidation can discard useful evidence without
any explicit deletion [Zhang et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib61), [Yang et al.
\[2026b\]](#bib.bib63). Where future usefulness is uncertain and
continued retention is permitted, reversible suppression or archival may
be preferable to irreversible removal. Maintenance should preserve
still-useful knowledge, reduce harmful influence, and retain enough
evidence to recover from a premature update or deletion. The next
question is whether the policies that make these choices can themselves
improve from feedback.

### 3.5 Feedback-Driven Policy Evolution

The preceding subsections describe how experience enters memory, how it
is organized and used, and how it is maintained as conditions change. We
now turn to the control of these operations, asking whether feedback can
change how the agent manages future experience.

##### Memory-State Adaptation versus Policy Evolution.

The state and policy updates in Equations
([3.1](#S3.E1 "Equation 3.1 ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))
and
([3.2](#S3.E2 "Equation 3.2 ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))
target different objects of adaptation. Persistent memory state
$`\mathcal{M}_{t}`$ can continually evolve even when the policy-update
rule $`\mathcal{U}`$ leaves $`\pi_{t}^{\mathrm{mem}}`$ static, and a
fixed policy can already select different operations across contexts. To
avoid conflating these mechanisms, we distinguish three levels at which
task feedback can enter the lifecycle: (1) *state-level incorporation*,
where feedback is written into $`\mathcal{M}_{t}`$ as new experience
under a fixed policy; (2) *offline policy training*, where downstream
task objectives optimize $`\pi^{\mathrm{mem}}`$ prior to deployment (as
in AgeMem and UMA [Yu et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib56), [Zhang et al.
\[2026e\]](#bib.bib64)), after which the learned controller remains
frozen; and (3) *online policy evolution*, where accumulated deployment
feedback continually modifies how future memory operations are selected
or configured ($`\pi_{t}^{\mathrm{mem}}\to\pi_{t+1}^{\mathrm{mem}}`$).
Online policy evolution is therefore an advanced layer of adaptation
rather than a prerequisite for all memory-centric continual learning.

##### Feedback as a Control Signal.

Existing systems illustrate how feedback operates across these levels.
At the state level, Reflexion stores verbal reflections derived from
task outcomes, and Voyager uses execution verification to admit
procedures into its skill library [Shinn et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib15),
[Wang et al. \[2024b\]](#bib.bib38); both improve subsequent behavior by
enriching $`\mathcal{M}_{t}`$ without altering the underlying memory
management rules. At the controller-training level, AgeMem and UMA
optimize memory-action selection against downstream rewards, while
VerMem supplies verifier-derived supervision for memory transitions
during training [Yu et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib56), [Zhang et al.
\[2026e\]](#bib.bib64), [Sun et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib65). The critical
distinction lies in *what* the feedback updates and *when* that update
occurs: storing feedback modifies available experience
($`\mathcal{M}_{t}`$), whereas training or adapting a controller
modifies the decision mechanism ($`\pi^{\mathrm{mem}}`$). Closing the
full policy-evolution loop requires keeping $`\pi_{t}^{\mathrm{mem}}`$
open to continual revision during deployment across formation,
consolidation, retrieval, and maintenance, rather than treating feedback
merely as a final post-processing step.

##### Policy-Level Stability–Plasticity.

When memory-management rules change, the stability–plasticity trade-off
also applies to the process that controls future learning. An overly
aggressive policy may repeatedly overwrite useful evidence, whereas an
overly conservative policy may reject experience needed for adaptation.
The effects of either policy can extend across many later memory
transitions. Learning from these effects is difficult because the value
of a write, consolidation, or deletion may become clear only in a later
task. A final task outcome may not reveal which earlier memory decision
was responsible. Verifier-based supervision can provide more local
signals during training, but it does not by itself establish the
long-term value of an operation [Sun et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib65).
Storage, latency, and computation costs must also be weighed against
future benefits. Repeated adaptation can erode previously useful
capabilities, which makes short-term gains an insufficient guide to
future updates [Yu et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib109). The objective is
therefore to improve memory management without repeatedly sacrificing
evidence or capabilities that remain useful.

Figure
[5](#S3.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ Policy-Level Stability–Plasticity. ‣ 3.5 Feedback-Driven Policy Evolution ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
brings the lifecycle operations and their control mechanisms into a
common view, mapping representative systems to the operations they
emphasize and the basis on which those decisions are controlled. Across
the horizontal axis, the figure groups operations into formation
(*selective writing*), integration (*organization and consolidation*),
utilization (*retrieval and utilization*), and maintenance, where
*revision* and *forgetting* are shown separately to distinguish the
mechanisms discussed in Section
[3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Revision and Forgetting ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents").
A single system may span several operations and combine different forms
of control.

![Refer to caption](2610.29357v1/memory_lifecycle_landscape.png)

Figure 5: Lifecycle–control landscape of selected representative agent
memory systems. The horizontal axis shows the memory operations
emphasized by each system. The vertical axis shows how decisions are
controlled: through prescribed rules, deliberative runtime LLM
reasoning, or learned control. Darker regions indicate the main design
focus and lighter regions indicate supporting operations. Colors
distinguish design families. Positions describe design choices, not
performance.

The landscape shows overlapping design priorities rather than a single
path of development. Generative Agents and MemoryBank emphasize
reflection, summarization, and the selective reuse of past experience
[Park et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib14), [Zhong et al.
\[2024\]](#bib.bib100). RecMem and LycheeMemory V2 make the timing and
unit of consolidation explicit through semantic recurrence and segment
boundaries [Dai et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib59), [Li et al.
\[2026a\]](#bib.bib58). SEEM, Zep (Graphiti), and A-MEM organize
evolving memory through event fusion and provenance, temporal fact
management, and note linking and updates, respectively [Lu et al.
\[2026\]](#bib.bib35), [Rasmussen et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib36), [Xu et
al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib18). MemGPT and Mem0 place selected decisions
about memory tiers or records under runtime deliberation [Packer et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib16), [Chhikara et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib17). AgeMem,
UMA, and MemRL train memory decisions or retrieval utilities against
downstream objectives [Yu et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib56), [Zhang et al.
\[2026e\]](#bib.bib64), [Zhang et al. \[2026g\]](#bib.bib125). These
emphases concern different parts of the learning process and can coexist
within a single system.

Two points guide the interpretation of this comparison. First, lifecycle
operation and control type are distinct design choices. LycheeMemory V2,
for example, combines prescribed segment-boundary rules with LLM-planned
retrieval [Li et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib58). A mechanism can respond to
changing inputs without its decision rule being learned from outcomes.
Second, similarly named operations may address different problems.
Compressing active context manages a short-term resource limit; the
consolidation discussed in Section
[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Organization, Compression, and Consolidation ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
organizes or transforms experience for persistent reuse. Likewise,
providing a Delete operation permits removal but does not establish that
the system chooses what to forget appropriately. The figure should
therefore be read as a map of design choices, not a count of solved
learning problems. The central challenge is to coordinate these choices
so that consolidation preserves evidence needed for later use and
maintenance reduces outdated influence without damaging valid knowledge.

Taken together, the lifecycle explains how experience can change
persistent memory, how retrieval and utilization connect that memory to
behavior, and how revision and forgetting maintain it as evidence
changes. Feedback may also change the policies controlling these
operations. Neither a memory update nor a policy update, however, is
sufficient evidence of successful continual learning. The next section
examines their behavioral consequences, asking whether experience
produces durable improvement, appropriate adaptation, and transfer while
preserving still-useful capabilities.

## 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners

Sections
[2](#S2 "2 A Unified Framework: Architecting Memory for Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
and
[3](#S3 "3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
specify how experience can persist, what reusable form it can take, and
how it is admitted, consolidated, used, revised, and governed. Those
design choices do not, by themselves, establish that continual learning
has occurred. A changed store, a retrieved record, or a higher
single-task score can all arise without a durable, experience-induced
change in later behavior. This section therefore treats evaluation as an
evidentiary problem, asking what empirical observations would justify a
continual-learning claim given the architecture and lifecycle
established in prior sections. We first specify three core behavioral
outcomes (retention, adaptation, and transfer) and then establish the
protocols needed to measure them without confounding memory with
expanded context windows, extra test-time computation, or transient,
isolated success.

### 4.1 What to Measure: Retention, Adaptation, and Transfer

Access-level metrics remain useful, but they answer a narrower question
than learning. Content correctness and retrieval scores diagnose whether
experience was written and can still be found; a single-task outcome
shows only that memory helped on the current probe (Table
[1](#S4.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 4.1 What to Measure: Retention, Adaptation, and Transfer ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
Continual learning additionally requires that an experiential gain
persist under delay and interference, remain revisable when the world
changes, and transfer beyond remembered episodes [Lopez-Paz and Ranzato
\[2017\]](#bib.bib107), [Wang et al. \[2024c\]](#bib.bib108). The
remainder of this subsection operationalizes those three outcomes.
Component traces localize failure; they do not substitute for the
behavioral claim.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  Metric Layer |  Demonstrated Capability |  Unaddressed Failure Mode |
|  Content Fidelity |  Accurate and lossless storage of past factual observations |  Does not guarantee that stored evidence will be retrieved or utilized in future decisions |
|  Retrieval Access |  High recall, precision, and reachability of relevant memories |  Does not prevent retrieved evidence from being ignored, misinterpreted, or misapplied |
|  Single-Task Outcome |  Immediate performance boost on a specific evaluation probe |  Does not establish retention under delay and interference, adaptive revision, or transfer |

Table 1: Diagnostic scope of evaluation metric layers in agent memory
systems. While component-level and snapshot metrics diagnose internal
storage fidelity and retrieval reachability, establishing continual
learning requires behavioral retention, adaptive revision, and
cross-task transfer.

#### 4.1.1 Retention: Learning Must Survive Delay and Interference

Section
[3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Retrieval and Utilization ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
distinguishes preservation, access, utilization, and behavioral effect
in the memory lifecycle. Retention evaluation uses the same cascade, but
changes the object of measurement from a stored trace to a capability
margin. After delay and subsequent experience, the agent should still
outperform a matched agent deprived of that experience on the same
probe. An entry can remain in a store, or a latent state can remain
decodable, while its contribution to later decisions has vanished.
Conversely, a useful procedure may persist without the originating
episode. Trace survival is therefore diagnostic; behavioral persistence
is the learning claim.

The four gates yield a testable *weakest-gate hypothesis*, positing that
behavioral retention is bottlenecked by the least reliable step and that
improving an earlier gate does not necessarily raise the final outcome.
Increasing capacity can raise preservation while lowering access through
denser retrieval competition. “The system remembers $`x`$” concerns
preservation and access; “the system retained learning from $`x`$”
additionally requires utilization and effect. With the base model fixed,
continual insertion and eviction in an external store can reduce
performance on earlier tasks [Yu et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib109), and
repeated extraction and injection of experience can turn a single-round
gain into progressive collapse [Chen et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib60).
These failures are not confined to catastrophic forgetting in weights,
as stored content can survive while remaining unaccessed, unused, or
ineffective.

Time and intervening experience are not interchangeable. A retention
protocol should first measure the immediate gain $`\Delta_{i}^{0}`$ on
capability $`i`$ relative to a memoryless or reset condition, then
remeasure after delay $`d`$ and interference load $`m`$. The normalized
retained gain $`\Delta_{i}(d,m)/\Delta_{i}^{0}`$, reported only when
$`\Delta_{i}^{0}>0`$, distinguishes failure to retain from failure to
learn. Delay should be specified in wall-clock time, turns, or token
distance; interference should report the number, semantic overlap, and
conflict relation of intervening updates. Substrate-specific traces then
localize the broken gate, identifying deletion, eviction, and rank
competition in external memory, or overwriting, state drift, and readout
mismatch in activation and parametric memory (Section
[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Memory Substrates: Physical Foundations for Stability and Plasticity ‣ 2 A Unified Framework: Architecting Memory for Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
These outcomes are the quantities against which capacity, interference,
and cost are traded in Section
[4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents").

#### 4.1.2 Adaptation: Learning Must Remain Revisable under Change

When facts, preferences, tools, or rules change, retention without
revision becomes a source of error. Section
[3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Revision and Forgetting ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
already treats conflict resolution, persistent update, and selective
forgetting as lifecycle operations. Evaluation asks a more targeted
question, examining whether behavior faithfully follows a valid update
after an environment change without indiscriminate damage to
still-useful capabilities. The relevant evaluation chain is temporal
rather than a single write (detection, revision, recovery, and
preservation), which should be quantified via detection delay, steps to
recovery, stale-error duration, and collateral degradation on unchanged
probes. Better recall does not automatically yield more
preference-aligned behavior [Xiao et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib111), and
summary memories can retain factual detail while losing the preference
signal needed after an environmental shift [Wang et al.
\[2026a\]](#bib.bib117).

Component diagnostics should follow the substrate. For external stores,
the question is whether the current record supersedes the stale one and
whether retrieval ranks it above obsolete evidence. Exposed maintenance
operations establish that updates are possible, but conversational QA
after a long history does not demonstrate recovery under controlled
drift [Chhikara et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib17). For parametric and latent
memories, injection accuracy and sequential fact updates are retention
and write diagnostics, not evidence that an acting agent detected an
unannounced change or resumed correct tool use [Wang et al.
\[2024e\]](#bib.bib12), [Das et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib114). Evaluated
models can verbalize that a fact has changed yet fail to propagate that
````
