````math
judgment to the final prediction [Wallat et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib112).
A credible adaptation claim therefore requires matched pre-change and
post-change probes, together with simultaneous reporting of recovery and
selective preservation.

#### 4.1.3 Transfer: Learning Must Extend beyond Remembered Episodes

Section
[2](#S2 "2 A Unified Framework: Architecting Memory for Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
classifies reusable experience as episodic, semantic, or procedural
assets. Transfer evaluation asks whether those assets support later
behavior beyond storing an answer. Three increasingly demanding cases
should be separated: replaying the same or a near-duplicate episode,
solving an unseen variant from the same task family, and applying shared
structure to a distinct target task. Source and target partitions must
exclude duplicate instances and target answers, document the shared
structure, such as a workflow, causal rule, tool convention, or
subskill, while keeping the base model, tools, context budget, and
compute matched. Otherwise nearest-neighbour reproduction and extra
context can be mistaken for learned transfer.

Abstraction directly supports reuse, as demonstrated by the successful
transfer of insights, workflows, and executable skills across related
tasks and environments [Zhao et al. \[2024a\]](#bib.bib34), [Wang et al.
\[2025c\]](#bib.bib39), [Wang et al. \[2024b\]](#bib.bib38). The same
evidence shows why mean target accuracy is insufficient. Raw trajectory
memory can produce negative forward transfer where abstract insights do
not, with harm concentrated on cases that a no-memory agent could not
already solve [Hu et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib115). Dedicated memory and
skill-maintenance methods are only selectively helpful and can be
matched or outperformed by ordinary in-context accumulation [Asawa et
al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib104), [Guan et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib41), [Zhong
et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib42). Transfer should therefore be reported as
zero-shot target gain or forward transfer, interactions or tokens to a
target threshold, and explicit negative-transfer rates [Lopez-Paz and
Ranzato \[2017\]](#bib.bib107), [Rodríguez et al.
\[2018\]](#bib.bib116). For external memory, analysis should identify
whether the retrieved asset is a case, rule, workflow, or skill, and
whether success depends on answer overlap. For neural memory, it should
test whether updated latent or parametric state improves a held-out
target without degrading unrelated tasks.

### 4.2 How to Measure: Protocols for Experience, Controls, and Time

#### 4.2.1 Benchmarks Must Contain Learnable Experience

A parallel distinction applies to benchmark design. The presence of
“history” does not imply the presence of learnable experience. Many
suites pre-supply dialogue logs, profiles, or knowledge bases and then
test whether related questions can be answered after the history is
given [Maharana et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib97), [Du et al.
\[2024\]](#bib.bib99), [Wu et al. \[2025a\]](#bib.bib98), [Zhong et al.
\[2024\]](#bib.bib100). Such protocols diagnose long-horizon access.
They usually cannot decide whether a new capability was formed *during*
evaluation.

Evidence strength rises with the experience that the protocol actually
exposes (Table
[2](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 4.2.1 Benchmarks Must Contain Learnable Experience ‣ 4.2 How to Measure: Protocols for Experience, Controls, and Time ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
Fixed history supports “remember and use.” Continual writing of state
supports “internal state accumulates.” Feedback that improves later
behavior, under the matched and sequential controls below, approaches
continual learning. Procedural streams ask whether success and failure
trajectories improve later workflows [Zheng et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib103); semantic and personalization streams ask whether
facts and preferences persist and can be revised [Maharana et al.
\[2024\]](#bib.bib97), [Wu et al. \[2025a\]](#bib.bib98), [Tan et al.
\[2025a\]](#bib.bib101); dynamic-rule streams ask whether invalidated
experience is corrected in time [Hu et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib102),
[Asawa et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib104). A benchmark may excel on one
stream while remaining silent on the others. Externally grounded
outcomes and user corrections also support stronger learning claims than
self-generated reflections, which can be stored and later retrieved as
if they were facts [Shinn et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib15), [Park et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib14).

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  Benchmark Family |  Evaluation Paradigm |  Supported Learning Claim |
|  LoCoMo [Maharana et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib97), LongMemEval [Wu et al. \[2025a\]](#bib.bib98), PerLTQA [Du et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib99), MemoryBank [Zhong et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib100) |  Static dialogue history with single-turn question answering |  Long-context factual recall, passive profile extraction, and needle retrieval |
|  MemBench [Tan et al. \[2025a\]](#bib.bib101) |  Multi-scenario synthetic memory evaluation |  Access efficiency, query latency, and retrieval accuracy across storage scales |
|  MemoryAgentBench [Hu et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib102) |  Incremental multi-turn ingestion with evolving facts |  Dynamic knowledge assimilation, working-memory updates, and explicit conflict resolution |
|  LifelongAgentBench [Zheng et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib103), CL-Bench [Asawa et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib104), AgentCL [Shu et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib110) |  Sequential interactive environments with task feedback |  Cumulative experience accumulation, behavioral retention, and stateful margin ($`\Delta P_{t}`$) over reset baselines |

Table 2: Categorization of representative agent memory benchmarks by
evaluation paradigm. Benchmarks are organized by the extent to which
their experimental design supports genuine learning-from-experience
claims rather than static information retrieval.

#### 4.2.2 Matched Comparisons Reveal What Memory Contributes

Even when performance rises across a task sequence, the gain cannot be
attributed to memory until alternative explanations are removed. The
backbone, a longer context, static retrieval, or additional inference
can produce similar effects. Figure
[6](#S4.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 4.2.2 Matched Comparisons Reveal What Memory Contributes ‣ 4.2 How to Measure: Protocols for Experience, Controls, and Time ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
isolates accumulated experience by comparing a stateful condition with a
reset condition on a matched task,

|     |                                                              |     |       |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \Delta P_{t}=P_{\mathrm{stateful}}(t)-P_{\mathrm{reset}}(t).
       ```                                                           |     | (4.1) |

$`\Delta P_{t}`$ is not a new composite metric. It denotes the
additional benefit associated with accumulated state under otherwise
matched conditions [Tan et al. \[2025a\]](#bib.bib101), [Hu et al.
\[2026b\]](#bib.bib102), [Wei et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib54).

![Refer to
caption](2610.29357v1/figures/matched_comparison_framework.png)

Figure 6: Matched comparison framework for isolating the contribution of
agent memory. The stateful condition retains accumulated experience,
whereas the reset condition removes experience-related state on a
matched task.
$`\Delta P_{t}=P_{\mathrm{stateful}}(t)-P_{\mathrm{reset}}(t)`$ is the
additional benefit associated with accumulated state. No-memory,
full-history, static RAG, and matched-compute baselines progressively
rule out alternative explanations.

Further baselines rule out further alternatives. A no-memory or reset
condition shows what the model can already do. A full-history context
baseline tests whether the benefit is simply more historical text; when
a memory mechanism outperforms a matched long-context condition, the
evidence favors organization or selective use rather than context
expansion alone [Wang et al. \[2026d\]](#bib.bib43), [Tan et al.
\[2025a\]](#bib.bib101). A static RAG baseline tests whether dynamic
accumulation adds anything beyond a fixed knowledge source. Matched
computation addresses extra inference. Fine-tuning, LoRA, and model
editing absorb experience through different substrates and should not be
treated as interchangeable with all forms of agent memory, though they
remain useful comparison points.

What “reset” means depends on the substrate (Section
[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Memory Substrates: Physical Foundations for Stability and Plasticity ‣ 2 A Unified Framework: Architecting Memory for Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
External stores admit write-off, read-off, and clear-store
interventions, which separate encoding, use, and overall value. For
activation or parametric memory, reset cannot simply mean deleting a
database; evaluation must instead distinguish online parameter updates,
the preservation of persistent state, and subsequent readout [Gutiérrez
et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib19). A stateful condition may also bundle
conversation history, tool state, and external memory, so a positive
$`\Delta P_{t}`$ need not identify which mechanism produced the gain. A
stronger design keeps the backbone, task input, context budget,
retrieval budget, and inference compute matched while varying only
experience-related state.

#### 4.2.3 Sequential Evaluation Separates Durable Learning from Transient Success

The retention and adaptation definitions above already require the same
capability to be measured across memory states. Chronological length
does not substitute for that invariance. Endpoint scores after a long
history show that information can be used, but not when the benefit
appeared or whether it disappeared and recovered [Maharana et al.
\[2024\]](#bib.bib97), [Wu et al. \[2025a\]](#bib.bib98). A short
protocol that probes a capability before acquisition, immediately
afterward, and after a controlled update is stronger evidence than a
long trajectory with a single terminal score.

Four observation regimes support progressively stronger claims.
*Snapshots* test a final memory state; *streams* report online reward on
changing tasks; *reprobes* repeat the same capability or a calibrated
task variant; and *interventional panels* combine reprobes with an event
ledger and matched stateful–reset trajectories. AgentCL and CL-Bench
illustrate the value of a stable observation target, where first-pass,
frozen-memory, and held-out probes systematically isolate acquisition,
reuse, and transfer [Shu et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib110), [Asawa et al.
\[2026\]](#bib.bib104). Between probes, the ledger should record elapsed
time, writes, semantic overlap, conflicts, feedback, tool or rule
changes, and retrieval or update cost. Delay plus overlapping updates
tests retention; changes in facts, preferences, or tools test
adaptation; held-out tasks that preserve latent structure test transfer.
Evidence for one evaluation setting should not be treated as validation
across all three.

A compact blueprint follows. Define capability anchors and measure them
before and immediately after acquisition; schedule checkpoints on a
declared delay–interference grid; run stateful and reset conditions with
identical backbone, tools, context, retrieval, and inference budgets;
and populate a matrix $`R_{t,i}`$ that re-evaluates capability $`i`$
after update $`t`$. Diagonal entries measure acquisition,
lower-triangular entries expose retention and backward influence, and
held-out variants test transfer. Reporting the event ledger beside this
matrix prevents apparent gains from obscuring task drift or hidden
computational overhead.

#### 4.2.4 Fragmented Benchmarks Limit Cross-Study Conclusions

Existing suites occupy different cells of this design rather than a
common scale. Access-oriented histories, incremental ingestion, and
sequential interactive tasks are not interchangeable measurements of the
same construct, and they rarely match context budgets, retrieval depth,
or inference compute. Numerical comparison across papers is therefore
unreliable unless the protocol specifies the behavioral outcome in
Section
[4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 What to Measure: Retention, Adaptation, and Transfer ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"),
the experience regime above, and the matched and sequential controls
that isolate memory. Scores should be read within that evidentiary
scope, not as a generic ranking of memory systems.

### 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency

#### 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute

Non-parametric memory makes deployment experience a resource for
continued capability growth with fixed model weights. Agents preserve
interaction facts [Chhikara et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib17), distill
reasoning strategies, and consolidate experience for later tasks [Ouyang
et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib40), [Yin et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib132). The
resulting capability depends on how much relevant experience is
available and how efficiently the memory system retains and uses it.

We propose a Memory Scaling Law relating attainable performance to
experience, persistent capacity, and read–write compute. Its central
prediction is a sequence of resource-limited regimes, where performance
improves as relevant experience accumulates and subsequently saturates
when capacity or read–write compute constrains its effective use.
Expanding the limiting resources shifts these saturation points. The
information structure of experience determines how quickly capacity
demand grows, while reuse determines how much computation is worth
investing in writing and consolidation. This view connects memory
representations and lifecycle operations to the rate and cost of
capability growth during deployment.

##### Resource dimensions and attainable performance.

Let $`E_{t}`$ denote cumulative experience, $`\bar{B}`$ the persistent
capacity limit, $`B_{t}`$ actual occupancy, $`R`$ the per-query read
budget, and $`U`$ the write and maintenance budget over the same
experience horizon. The budget $`U`$ covers extraction, indexing,
revision, and consolidation. These quantities distinguish what an agent
encounters, what it retains, and what it can consult for a decision.
Equal text volumes can contain different amounts of task-relevant
information, so a scaling relation also depends on the source and
composition of experience.

For a fixed foundation model $`\theta`$, task distribution
$`\mathcal{D}`$, experience-generation process, and remaining inference
settings, let $`\epsilon^{\star}(E,\bar{B},R,U\mid\theta,\mathcal{D})`$
be the minimum expected task error within a specified class of memory
policies. Task error is an error rate or a gap in normalized task score.
This attainable frontier separates resource limits from the performance
of an individual method. Capacity accounts for all persistent
experience-dependent state, including informative vectors and indices;
text-token occupancy measures its textual part.

##### Joint scaling and resource-optimal allocation.

Within a regime of relatively stable experience quality and task
structure, we hypothesize

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\epsilon^{\star}-\epsilon_{\infty}\asymp\max_{j\in\mathcal{A}}a_{j}x_{j}^{-\alpha_{j}},\qquad\mathbf{x}=\left(\frac{E}{E_{0}},\frac{\bar{B}}{B_{0}},\frac{R}{R_{0}},\frac{U}{U_{0}}\right),\quad a_{j},\alpha_{j}>0.
``` |  | (4.2) |

The model and task family determine the limiting error
$`\epsilon_{\infty}`$;
$`\epsilon_{\mathrm{ex}}=\epsilon^{\star}-\epsilon_{\infty}`$ denotes
excess task error. Each dimensionless $`x_{j}`$ measures scale relative
to a reference budget, and $`\mathcal{A}`$ contains the active resource
constraints. The largest term approximates the error from the dominant
shortage; an additive form gives a smooth approximation when several
terms are comparable. The exponents and the ranges over which they apply
are empirical quantities.

A long-tailed demand distribution gives a simple capacity example.
Suppose each query requires one knowledge unit, unit demand satisfies
$`p_{i}\propto i^{-s}`$ with $`s>1`$, and each unit occupies $`\tau`$
tokens. If the experience pool contains all units and the most requested
$`K=\lfloor\bar{B}/\tau\rfloor`$ units are stored and reliably accessed,
uncovered demand is
$`\epsilon_{\mathrm{cov}}(\bar{B})=\sum_{i>K}p_{i}\propto\bar{B}^{-(s-1)}`$.
Then $`\alpha_{B}=s-1`$: the capacity exponent follows from the
distribution of task-relevant demand under these assumptions.

A capacity-only sweep can plateau because reading, experience supply, or
maintenance becomes limiting. Expanding the limiting resource moves the
plateau, allowing further gains from capacity (Figure
[7](#S4.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ Joint scaling and resource-optimal allocation. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
The joint relation thus predicts both diminishing returns within a fixed
configuration and renewed improvement when resource allocation changes.
Keeping the active experience, capacity, and reading terms at comparable
orders gives a balanced scaling path:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\frac{\bar{B}}{B_{0}}\propto\left(\frac{E}{E_{0}}\right)^{\alpha_{E}/\alpha_{B}},\qquad\frac{R}{R_{0}}\propto\left(\frac{E}{E_{0}}\right)^{\alpha_{E}/\alpha_{R}}.
``` |  | (4.3) |

Maintenance follows an analogous relation. Resources need not grow in
equal proportions. Under a lifecycle budget $`C`$ and $`N`$ reuses, the
cost function $`\mathcal{C}_{N}`$ also determines the optimal
allocation:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbf{x}^{\star}(C,N)\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\mathbf{x}:\,\mathcal{C}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\leq C}\epsilon^{\star}(\mathbf{x}\mid\theta,\mathcal{D}).
``` |  | (4.4) |

The frontier is specific to the policy class: admitting more efficient
representations or access mechanisms can change its shape and its
optimal allocation. Foundation-model capabilities also condition the
returns. Pretrained knowledge reduces some storage requirements, while
the ability to interpret abstractions and combine evidence increases the
value of a given memory bank. Recent facts and private interactions have
to be acquired during deployment.

![Refer to caption](2610.29357v1/x1.png)

Figure 7: Theoretical illustration of joint scaling and optimal resource
allocation. (a) Fixed-capacity error curves and their optimized lower
envelope. (b) Returns at equal total budgets. (c) Optimal capacity and
reading budgets. (d) Equal-error contours and the optimal path. For
normalized capacity $`b=\bar{B}/B_{0}`$ and reading $`r=R/R_{0}`$,
curves use $`\epsilon_{\mathrm{ex}}(b,r)=0.6b^{-0.55}+0.4r^{-0.75}`$ and
$`C=b+r`$, with other resources non-binding. Filled markers sample this
function; hollow diamonds mark its calculated optima.

The measured comparisons in Table
[3](#S4.T3 "Table 3 ‣ Experience structure and capacity requirements. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
use Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507 for construction, answering, and local
model-based scoring, with 1,024-dimensional qwen3-embedding-0.6b
embeddings. We evaluate Mem0 0.1.94 [Chhikara et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib17) and retrieval over retained raw histories (Raw
RAG). Capacity sweeps use frozen bank subsets; continual tasks update
memory under a capacity limit. Stored and read text is counted with
o200k_base. Scores and occupancy are macro-averaged over conversations
or samples, with cluster-bootstrap $`95\%`$ intervals. LongMemEval uses
a local semantic judge, and MemoryBench uses local rubric scoring.

While the additive approximation in Figure
[7](#S4.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ Joint scaling and resource-optimal allocation. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
predicts that storage and reading co-scale under a shared budget
constraint, empirical history-based question answering also exhibits
direct interaction because effective readout is bounded by retained
storage ($`R_{\mathrm{eff}}=\min(R,B_{t})`$) and retrieval competition
(Figure
[8](#S4.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ Joint scaling and resource-optimal allocation. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(a–c)).
On LoCoMo [Maharana et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib97), increasing the read
budget from 512 to 2,048 tokens improves Raw RAG F1 by 0.89 percentage
points at one-eighth history retention and 3.82 points at full
retention. The paired difference between these gains is 2.93 points
($`95\%`$ interval: $`[2.06,4.02]`$). On the selected LongMemEval [Wu et
al. \[2025a\]](#bib.bib98) samples, full-history Raw RAG accuracy
increases from 35.00% to 44.00% over the same read-budget change. These
observations confirm storage–reading complementarity: the return from
additional reading depends on the available memory.

Figure 8: Measured performance across capacity, reading, and experience
settings. (a–b) Stored text and Raw RAG performance on LoCoMo and
LongMemEval. (c) Paired gains from increasing LoCoMo reading from
$`512`$ to $`2{,}048`$ tokens; error bars in (a–c) are cluster-bootstrap
$`95\%`$ intervals. (d) Stateful–reset score differences on fixed
IdeaBench questions at successive checkpoints, without periodic
consolidation; shading spans the min–max range across three random
training-dialogue orderings. Continuous horizontal axes report
text-token volumes; (c) indexes retained-history fractions, and capacity
labels in (d) denote upper limits.

##### Experience structure and capacity requirements.

During deployment, experience can introduce new task demands, so the
relevant distribution evolves as $`\mathcal{D}_{E}`$. At fixed capacity,
early gains can give way to decline when the information needed to serve
this expanding workload outgrows the bank (Figure
[9](#S4.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ Experience structure and capacity requirements. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(a)).
For $`n`$ independent, equiprobable binary facts that are independent of
the fixed model and queried uniformly, keeping average reconstruction
error below $`\delta<1/2`$ requires at least $`n[1-h_{2}(\delta)]`$
bits, where $`h_{2}`$ is binary entropy. Shared rules and skills permit
more compact representations; a finite set of recurring task structures
can produce saturating demand.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
| Empirical Study |  Experimental Protocol & Controls |  Measured Outcomes |
| LoCoMo Capacity Sweep |  10 long-form dialogues, 1,540 questions; $`4\times 4`$ storage–read capacity grid |  Lexical F1 score with cluster-bootstrap 95% CI |
| LongMemEval Capacity Sweep |  100 selected samples; storage–read capacity grid |  Local semantic accuracy |
| IdeaBench Sequential Deployment |  50 fixed target queries evaluated across sequential training checkpoints (3 random task orderings) |  Stateful–reset performance margin ($`\Delta P_{t}`$) without consolidation |
| LoCoMo Query Economics |  Cohort of 9 conversations, 180 questions; comparing direct read, search, and search + summarize |  F1 score, serialized context tokens, and end-to-end query latency |

Table 3: Experimental comparisons in this section. Repeated predictions
across budgets are aggregated within the stated sampling units. LoCoMo
scores use categories 1–4.

Let $`B_{\mathrm{req}}(E;p_{0})`$ be the minimum capacity needed to
maintain performance $`p_{0}`$ as relevant task demand expands with
experience. With adequate reading and maintenance, we describe its
growth within a stable regime as

|     |                                           |     |       |
|-----|-------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       B_{\mathrm{req}}(E;p_{0})\propto E^{\nu}.
       ```                                        |     | (4.5) |

The exponent $`\nu`$ describes capacity demand, while $`\alpha_{j}`$
describes the performance return from a resource. Independent
information, shared structure, and recurring rules yield different
demand curves (Figure
[9](#S4.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ Experience structure and capacity requirements. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(b)).
A system that preserves separate episodes and one that extracts reusable
rules can therefore require different capacity growth to sustain the
same target performance.

Consolidation changes this relationship by turning repeated detail into
reusable structure. ReasoningBank converts successes and failures into
reasoning strategies [Ouyang et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib40). Experience
Scaling merges and refines experience to reduce redundancy, while
finding that excessive compression can harm performance [Yin et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib132). Abstractions can support multiple tasks, while
exceptions and rare queries may need the original details. Episodic,
semantic, and procedural assets thus have different fidelity and
capacity requirements. Under environmental change, the rate of revision
also determines how quickly useful performance can recover (Figure
[9](#S4.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ Experience structure and capacity requirements. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(c)).

Figure 9: Theoretical illustration of experience structure, capacity
requirements, and adaptation. Curves use preset response and recovery
functions. (a) Performance as task demand expands with experience, at
fixed capacity limits $`\bar{B}`$. (b) Capacity needed to maintain a
target under different experience structures. (c) Recovery after
environmental changes, marked by dashed lines; $`m`$ is the maintenance
rate.

Across the evaluated IdeaBench checkpoints from MemoryBench [Ai et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib113), Mem0’s stateful–reset score difference varies
non-monotonically (Figure
[8](#S4.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ Joint scaling and resource-optimal allocation. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(d);
Mem0 is run in its default online mode, which also writes during the
50-query evaluation pass and produces small non-zero offsets at
$`t=0`$). At an 8,192-token capacity limit and $`R=1{,}024`$, mean
stateful–reset gain falls from $`+7.10`$ percentage points after 64
training dialogues to $`-10.43`$ after 128 and $`-13.93`$ at the final
256-dialogue checkpoint (because unconstrained occupancy plateaus at
3,858 tokens, neither the 8,192- nor the 32,768-token cap binds; pooling
both 3-ordering replicates gives $`+3.18`$ points at 64 dialogues,
$`-10.38`$ at 128, and $`-11.60`$ at 256). Consolidation every 16 or 64
training dialogues reduces final occupancy from 3,858 to approximately
1,800 tokens, while final gains remain negative.

##### Selective access and the economics of reuse.

Selective access allows persistent memory and per-query input to scale
separately. If a query needs a bounded number of fixed-length records
and indexing reliably locates them, model input can stay bounded as the
bank grows. Tasks that synthesize more evidence may require more
reading, and index search and updates still incur costs. Cross-task
reuse can shift work from repeated reading and reasoning to earlier
extraction or consolidation. With costs expressed in consistent units,
average cost over $`N`$ reuses is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\overline{C}_{N}=\frac{C_{\mathrm{build}}+C_{\mathrm{maint}}(N)}{N}+\overline{C}_{\mathrm{query}}.
``` |  | (4.6) |

At equal performance, lightweight writing and direct reading may cost
less when reuse is limited. More expensive preparation becomes
worthwhile when cumulative query savings exceed its additional cost. For
fixed-quality policies with no ongoing maintenance, the crossover reuse
count is the extra construction cost divided by the per-query saving.

Figure 10: Theoretical illustration of reuse and resource-optimal policy
choice. (a) Query-cost–performance Pareto frontiers; lighter points
denote dominated configurations. (b) Amortization at a common
performance target $`P=0.75`$. (c) The best policy at each reuse count
and average-cost budget; gray denotes infeasibility, and dashed lines
are performance contours. Preset functions specify policy performance
and costs in common arbitrary units, with offline consolidation and no
ongoing maintenance.

Token savings and latency savings can differ. On the matched LoCoMo
small-bank cohort (Table
[4](#S4.T4 "Table 4 ‣ Selective access and the economics of reuse. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")),
query-time summarization reduces serialized memory input but increases
latency. Its paired F1 is $`0.72`$ percentage points below full-bank
reading ($`95\%`$ interval for the difference: $`[-1.14,-0.27]`$).
Policy choice consequently depends on which resource is constrained and
the performance target.

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| Reader | F1 (%) $`\uparrow`$ | 95% interval | Input (tokens) | Latency (s) $`\downarrow`$ |
| Read all | 4.63 | \[3.28, 6.02\] | 729.78 | 0.154 |
| Search | 4.29 | \[3.15, 5.50\] | 225.68 | 0.154 |
| Search + summarize | 3.91 | \[2.81, 5.15\] | 43.52 | 0.367 |

Table 4: Three reading policies over Mem0 memory banks on nine LoCoMo
conversations and 180 questions, with three timing repetitions. Memory
input includes serialization and formatting. Median latency covers
retrieval, query-time summarization, and answering, excluding offline
construction. F1 intervals use $`10{,}000`$ conversation-cluster
bootstrap resamples.

Environmental change bounds how many times a memory can be reused before
it needs revision. Retention extends the period over which preparation
costs can be amortized, and transfer increases the number of tasks
served by the same experience. Adaptation incurs the maintenance cost
required to keep that experience useful. These continual-learning
properties determine the reuse horizon and update costs in the
resource-optimal allocation of Equation
([4.4](#S4.E4 "Equation 4.4 ‣ Joint scaling and resource-optimal allocation. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).

#### 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law

Parametric memory writes deployment experience into model parameters or
persistent neural states rather than into an external store.
Representative designs include trainable memory layers [Berges et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib163), plug-and-play memory modules [Cao et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib164), memory states updated at test time [Behrouz et
al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib23), [Kuratov et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib169), side
memories isolated from the backbone [Wang et al. \[2024d\]](#bib.bib21),
[Das et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib114), [Wang et al. \[2024e\]](#bib.bib12),
and direct editing or fine-tuning of backbone weights [Meng et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib165), [Xu et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib168). These
approaches confront the same fundamental question as non-parametric
memory regarding how performance scales with experience and resources,
and at what write and compute cost. They differ in one essential
respect. All writes share the same parameters, so new writes alter how
earlier content is read out. Accumulating experience therefore adds
usable content while simultaneously increasing the probability that
existing content is overwritten. We build this property into the scaling
relation and propose a parametric counterpart to Equation
([4.2](#S4.E2 "Equation 4.2 ‣ Joint scaling and resource-optimal allocation. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).

##### Resource dimensions of parametric memory.

We retain the notation of Section
[4.3.1](#S4.SS3.SSS1 "4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"),
where $`E_{t}`$ denotes cumulative experience, $`\theta`$ denotes the
foundation model, and $`\mathcal{D}`$ denotes the task distribution.
Parametric memory involves three resources. Memory capacity $`M`$ is the
number of memory parameters, memory slots, adapter rank, or
persistent-state dimensions, playing the role of $`\bar{B}`$. The write
budget $`U`$ represents the computation used to write experience into
parameters, such as gradient steps, update steps per write, and write
frequency. Readout compute is fixed by the architecture, as each forward
pass traverses the memory parameters or performs a fixed top-$`k`$
lookup without consuming additional prompt context tokens; consequently,
the read budget $`R`$ is generally not an independent decision variable.
This marks the first major divergence from non-parametric memory.

The second difference concerns occupancy. External memory can directly
report its actual occupancy $`B_{t}`$, whereas parametric memory has no
directly observable counterpart. We therefore define the load ratio

|     |                                     |     |       |
|-----|-------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \rho_{t}=\frac{I(E_{t})}{\kappa M},
       ```                                  |     | (4.7) |

where $`I(E_{t})`$ is the information in the experience that must be
retained, measured in bits, and $`\kappa`$ is the information a single
parameter can store. Knowledge-capacity studies estimate
$`\kappa\approx 2`$ bits per parameter and report that this ratio holds
even under int8 quantization [Allen-Zhu and Li \[2025\]](#bib.bib162).
The dimensionless quantity $`\rho_{t}`$ measures how full the memory has
become.

##### Joint scaling with an interference term.

Within a regime of relatively stable experience quality and task
structure, we hypothesize

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\epsilon^{\star}-\epsilon_{\infty}\asymp\max_{j\in\mathcal{A}}a_{j}x_{j}^{-\alpha_{j}}+I_{s,\pi}(\rho_{t}),\qquad\mathbf{x}=\left(\frac{E}{E_{0}},\frac{M}{M_{0}},\frac{U}{U_{0}}\right).
``` |  | (4.8) |

The first term mirrors Equation
([4.2](#S4.E2 "Equation 4.2 ‣ Joint scaling and resource-optimal allocation. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")),
approximating the residual loss from resource shortages where the
largest component identifies the active bottleneck. The second term is
an interference term specific to parametric memory. It is close to zero
while the load ratio stays below a critical value
$`\rho^{\star}_{s,\pi}`$; beyond that value, new writes begin to
overwrite earlier content and loss rises. The critical ratio depends on
the memory substrate $`s`$ (Section
[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Memory Substrates: Physical Foundations for Stability and Plasticity ‣ 2 A Unified Framework: Architecting Memory for Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))
and on the write policy $`\pi`$. In non-parametric memory, additional
experience can crowd retrieval or trigger eviction while leaving
surviving records intact, a cost mediated by the read and maintenance
terms. In parametric memory, additional experience rewrites shared
parameters directly, so performance degradation as experience grows is
an intrinsic property of the substrate rather than only a retrieval or
selection problem.

Equation
([4.8](#S4.E8 "Equation 4.8 ‣ Joint scaling with an interference term. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))
yields three testable predictions. First, when experience and write
budget are fixed and only capacity grows, loss is initially dominated by
interference and follows the power law only after the load ratio enters
the safe regime (Figure
[11](#S4.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ Joint scaling with an interference term. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(a)).
Second, when capacity is fixed and experience grows, loss rises once the
load ratio crosses $`\rho^{\star}`$, and the transition can differ by
orders of magnitude across substrates (Figure
[11](#S4.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ Joint scaling with an interference term. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(b)).
Third, load curves measured at several capacities should approximately
collapse onto a single curve when plotted against $`\rho`$. This
collapse is the most direct test of whether interference depends on load
only through the ratio.

Figure
[11](#S4.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ Joint scaling with an interference term. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")
illustrates these predictions with a minimal instance of Equation
([4.8](#S4.E8 "Equation 4.8 ‣ Joint scaling with an interference term. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
It keeps only the capacity term and the interference term,
$`\epsilon^{\star}-\epsilon_{\infty}=a_{M}M^{-\alpha_{M}}+I(\rho)`$,
with
$`I(\rho)=\lambda(\rho/\rho^{\star})^{b}/[1+(\rho/\rho^{\star})^{b}]`$
and $`\rho=E/M`$ in relative units ($`\kappa=1`$). We set
$`a_{M}=\alpha_{M}=0.5`$, $`\lambda=0.6`$, and $`b=2`$. Backbone editing
and a dedicated module differ only in the critical ratio,
$`\rho^{\star}=3`$ and $`\rho^{\star}=300`$, while panel (a) uses
$`\rho^{\star}=1`$. Panel (c) plots
$`M_{\mathrm{req}}=E^{\nu}/\rho^{\star}`$ from Equation
([4.9](#S4.E9 "Equation 4.9 ‣ Capacity demand and balanced growth. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))
with $`\nu\in\{1,0.6\}`$.

Figure 11: Theoretical illustration of the parametric memory scaling
relation. (a) Excess loss versus memory capacity at different experience
volumes; interference dominates until the load ratio falls below
$`\rho^{\star}`$, after which the capacity term follows a power law. (b)
Excess loss versus accumulated experience at fixed capacity for backbone
editing (low $`\rho^{\star}`$) and a dedicated memory module (high
$`\rho^{\star}`$); dotted lines mark the critical loads. (c) Capacity
required to maintain a target performance for independent facts
($`\nu=1`$) and shared structure ($`\nu<1`$). Curves sample the
illustrative instance of Equations
([4.7](#S4.E7 "Equation 4.7 ‣ Resource dimensions of parametric memory. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))–([4.9](#S4.E9 "Equation 4.9 ‣ Capacity demand and balanced growth. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))
described in the text.

##### Evidence for the capacity term.

Experiments that hold the foundation model fixed and enlarge only the
memory parameters support the shape of the capacity term. Memory Layers
at Scale enlarges the Memory+ value count from 1M to 64M in a 1.3B
configuration (Figure
[12](#S4.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ Evidence for the load term and the critical ratio. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(a))
[Berges et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib163). NaturalQuestions accuracy rises
from 13.68 to 20.78 and TriviaQA F1 from 42.89 to 62.14, well above a
dense model of the same configuration without memory layers (7.76 and
32.64). Between 16M and 64M, however, NaturalQuestions improves by only
0.64 while TriviaQA still improves by 3.47, indicating that the same
capacity range places different tasks at different points on the power
law. The same work scales memory parameters to 128B on foundation models
of at most 8B, showing that memory capacity can far exceed backbone size
(though these Memory+ layers are trained jointly during pretraining, so
sequential deployment writes remain to be tested separately).

Direct functional evidence comes from LoRA memorization. Treating LoRA
rank $`r`$ as a capacity probe, a recent study reports a power law
$`\Delta\mathcal{L}=C\,r^{\alpha}\ell^{-\beta}+b`$ that links the loss
reduction to rank and sequence length $`\ell`$ across models, datasets,
and semantic densities ($`R^{2}>0.98`$) [Xu et al.
\[2026b\]](#bib.bib168). The relation contains both a capacity variable
and a load variable. If $`\alpha\approx\beta`$, it reduces to a function
of $`r/\ell`$, the dependence on the load ratio that Equation
([4.8](#S4.E8 "Equation 4.8 ‣ Joint scaling with an interference term. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))
predicts; whether the two exponents coincide is therefore a direct test
of the proposed form. The same study shows that capacity alone does not
determine what is stored. At equal rank on Qwen3-8B, memorization
accuracy differs sharply across write methods (Figure
[12](#S4.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ Evidence for the load term and the critical ratio. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(b)),
where at rank 128, SFT reaches $`40.0\%`$, MemFT-OT reaches $`100.0\%`$,
and MemFT-SW reaches $`94.4\%`$. In the terms of Equation
([4.8](#S4.E8 "Equation 4.8 ‣ Joint scaling with an interference term. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")),
the write method changes $`a_{M}`$ and $`\alpha_{M}`$, so better methods
lie closer to the attainable frontier at the same capacity.

The foundation model also conditions memory returns. Attaching the same
0.5B Memory Decoder to Qwen2.5 models from 0.5B to 72B reduces average
domain perplexity by 10.24 at 0.5B but by only 2.39 at 72B, and the 0.5B
model with the memory module (4.06) outperforms the 72B model alone
(5.85; Figure
[13](#S4.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ Write policy and lifecycle cost. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(a))
[Cao et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib164). This mirrors the substitution and
complementarity between model and memory discussed in Section
[4.3.1](#S4.SS3.SSS1 "4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents").
Continual fine-tuning studies further find that, between 1B and 7B
parameters, larger models forget more severely [Luo et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib32). The foundation model may therefore shift
$`\rho^{\star}`$ as well as $`\epsilon_{\infty}`$ and should be reported
as a controlled condition.

##### Evidence for the load term and the critical ratio.

Figure
[12](#S4.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ Evidence for the load term and the critical ratio. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(d)
summarizes the number of updates or evaluated write horizons reported
across representative parametric-memory studies. With direct backbone
editing, sequential ROME edits degrade performance after roughly 100 to
1,000 edits across typical edit sequences and can even trigger immediate
model collapse after certain individual disabling edits [Meng et al.
\[2022\]](#bib.bib170), [Gupta et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib166). Sequential
MEMIT enters catastrophic forgetting after about 1,400 edits on GPT-J
6B, whereas batch MEMIT on the same model edits 10,000 facts
simultaneously in a single batch [Gupta et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib166),
[Meng et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib165). When writes are isolated from the
backbone, Larimar sustains 1,000 sequential scope writes (200 facts
across 5 rephrasings) [Das et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib114), WISE remains
effective at 3,000 sequential edits on ZsRE without backbone collapse
[Wang et al. \[2024d\]](#bib.bib21), and MEMORYLLM, with a fixed memory
pool of about 1.07B parameters, maintains stable readout of newly
injected context across 650,000 sequential context updates under random
memory-slot replacement and exponential decay [Wang et al.
\[2024e\]](#bib.bib12). Because MEMORYLLM evaluates retention of recent
context under continual slot turnover rather than simultaneous retention
of all historical writes, and an update writes a context segment rather
than a single fact edit, only coarse orders of magnitude and protocol
regimes are comparable. Even so, the gap of roughly one to two orders of
magnitude between dedicated modules and sequential backbone editing, and
the gap between batch and sequential writes, are consistent with a
critical ratio shaped by substrate and policy.

The functional form of the interference term also has direct support.
Forgetting during LoRA fine-tuning follows a shifted power law in the
number of fine-tuned parameters and update steps, and neither early
stopping nor fewer trainable parameters avoids it [Kalajdzievski
\[2024\]](#bib.bib167). This motivates a power-law form for
$`I_{s,\pi}`$ as a starting point, to be tested for dependence on the
load ratio alone.

Write budget can partly substitute for capacity. GradMem fixes the
memory at eight vectors and increases the number of key–value pairs to
be stored (Figure
[12](#S4.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ Evidence for the load term and the critical ratio. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(c))
[Kuratov et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib169). With one write step, exact match
falls from $`100.0\%`$ at 4 pairs to $`32.6\%`$ at 96 pairs; with five
write steps, it remains $`99.1\%`$ at 64 pairs and $`88.4\%`$ at 96
pairs. In Equation
([4.8](#S4.E8 "Equation 4.8 ‣ Joint scaling with an interference term. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")),
the write budget $`U`$ moves the critical ratio to the right. This
result comes from a synthetic task, and existing studies vary either
capacity or load but not both, so the third prediction, collapse of load
curves across capacities, remains untested.

Figure 12: Reported capacity and load effects in published
parametric-memory studies. (a) Memory+ value count at a fixed 1.3B
configuration [Berges et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib163); dotted lines show
the dense model without memory layers. (b) Memorization accuracy versus
LoRA rank for three write methods on Qwen3-8B [Xu et al.
\[2026b\]](#bib.bib168). (c) Reported exact match versus stored
key–value pairs with eight fixed memory vectors and different numbers of
write steps [Kuratov et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib169). (d) Reported
sequential or batch update horizons across parametric-memory studies
[Meng et al. \[2022\]](#bib.bib170), [Meng et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib165), [Gupta et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib166), [Das et
al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib114), [Wang et al. \[2024d\]](#bib.bib21), [Wang
et al. \[2024e\]](#bib.bib12); MEMORYLLM evaluates recent-context
retention across 650,000 context updates under random slot replacement
rather than simultaneous retention of all past edits. All values are
taken from the original papers without fitting.

##### Capacity demand and balanced growth.

Analogous to Equation
([4.5](#S4.E5 "Equation 4.5 ‣ Experience structure and capacity requirements. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")),
maintaining a target performance $`p_{0}`$ requires the load ratio to
stay below its critical value, which implies

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
M_{\mathrm{req}}(E;p_{0})\gtrsim\frac{I(E)}{\kappa\,\rho^{\star}_{s,\pi}}\propto\frac{E^{\nu}}{\rho^{\star}_{s,\pi}}.
``` |  | (4.9) |

The exponent $`\nu`$ has the same meaning as in Equation
([4.5](#S4.E5 "Equation 4.5 ‣ Experience structure and capacity requirements. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")),
with $`\nu\approx 1`$ when experience consists of independent facts and
$`\nu<1`$ when it shares rules or skills. Parametric memory compresses
shared regularities into the same parameters as it writes, so
consolidation happens implicitly during training. Equation
([4.9](#S4.E9 "Equation 4.9 ‣ Capacity demand and balanced growth. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))
leads to two differences from non-parametric memory. First, when
external capacity falls short, degradation arises through retrieval
competition or eviction while surviving records remain intact; when
parametric capacity falls short, earlier content is directly overwritten
in shared weights, which is harder to reverse. Second, $`\rho^{\star}`$
appears in the denominator, so improving the substrate or the write
policy can reduce the required capacity by orders of magnitude (Figure
[11](#S4.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ Joint scaling with an interference term. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(c)),
often more efficiently than adding parameters. Within this constraint,
the balanced growth path follows Equation
([4.3](#S4.E3 "Equation 4.3 ‣ Joint scaling and resource-optimal allocation. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")),
with $`M/M_{0}\propto(E/E_{0})^{\alpha_{E}/\alpha_{M}}`$.

##### Write policy and lifecycle cost.

Writing more often is not always better. TMEM varies how much context
accumulates before a parametric write is triggered, where F1 peaks at a
4K-token threshold on LoCoMo (25.72) and at 12K on LongMemEval-S
(41.24), and declines at both smaller and larger thresholds (Figure
[13](#S4.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ Write policy and lifecycle cost. ‣ 4.3.2 Parametric Memory Scaling Law ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")(b–c))
[Ren et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib24). A plausible interpretation is that
writing too often produces fragmented updates and raises the effective
load quickly, whereas writing too rarely forces each update to compress
too much context at once. The policy $`\pi`$ therefore exhibits a
task-dependent favorable range rather than shifting the critical ratio
monotonically.

Figure 13: Reported effects of the foundation model and write timing on
parametric memory. (a) Average domain perplexity (biomedicine, finance,
and law) of Qwen2.5 models from 0.5B to 72B, with and without the same
0.5B Memory Decoder [Cao et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib164); arrows mark the
reduction at the smallest and largest models, and the dotted line marks
the 72B model alone. (b–c) F1 and exact match of TMEM on Qwen3-4B as the
context threshold that triggers a parametric write varies, on LoCoMo and
LongMemEval-S [Ren et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib24); hollow markers denote
the observed maxima. A larger threshold means less frequent writes, not
more write compute. All values are taken from the original papers
without fitting.

Under the cost structure of Equation
([4.6](#S4.E6 "Equation 4.6 ‣ Selective access and the economics of reuse. ‣ 4.3.1 Non-Parametric Memory Scaling Law: Experience, Capacity, and Compute ‣ 4.3 The Memory Scaling Law: Capacity, Interference, and Learning Efficiency ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")),
parametric memory concentrates cost in $`C_{\mathrm{build}}`$ and
$`C_{\mathrm{maint}}`$, through gradient updates and batch
consolidation, while context-token readout overhead in
$`\overline{C}_{\mathrm{query}}`$ is zero because readout happens within
the forward pass and consumes no additional prompt context. Its average
cost advantage therefore grows with the reuse count $`N`$. Under
environmental change, however, parametric memory cannot precisely delete
or revise an individual item, and each revision adds to the load, which
raises maintenance cost and directly affects adaptation (Section
[4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 What to Measure: Retention, Adaptation, and Transfer ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
This suggests a natural division of labor, where rapidly changing
experience is retained in external memory, while stable, frequently
reused experience is consolidated into parameters, in line with the
synergy discussed in Section
[6.3](#S6.SS3 "6.3 Synergistic Continual Learning through Experience Memory and Parametric Learning ‣ 6 Open Challenges in Memory-Centric Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents").

Parametric and non-parametric memory thus share resource-limited
power-law terms and an attainable frontier, but parametric memory
differs in three respects: its read budget is fixed by the architecture,
its scaling relation contains an interference term governed by the load
ratio, and its capacity demand is amplified by the critical ratio.
Scaling parametric memory is therefore not only a matter of increasing
$`M`$; isolated writes, batch writes, larger write budgets, and
well-chosen write timing are needed to keep $`\rho`$ below
$`\rho^{\star}`$.

## 5 Memory-Centric Continual Learning Applications Across Digital and Physical Domains

When agents deploy over weeks or months, their operational context
constantly shifts: user goals change, software interfaces update, and
physical surroundings evolve. In these settings, continual learning
serves two practical roles: it keeps long-running tasks on track, and it
applies past experience to new problems. Memory provides the backbone
for both. By storing past interactions, execution outcomes, and
environmental states, memory gives agents a stable basis for future
decisions.

These capabilities take four main forms across digital and physical
domains (Figure
[14](#S5.F14 "Figure 14 ‣ 5 Memory-Centric Continual Learning Applications Across Digital and Physical Domains ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")):
(1) personal agents track evolving user preferences; (2) digital agents
turn execution logs into reusable workflows; (3) embodied robots map
environments and acquire physical skills; and (4) multi-agent teams
share project context and collaboration patterns. Below, we examine how
each setting turns accumulated experience into durable capability.

![Refer to caption](2610.29357v1/applications_overview.png)

Figure 14: Memory-centric continual learning across application domains.
Each row shows how accumulated experience updates memory to support
personalized service, digital automation, embodied exploration, or
multi-agent collaboration. Return arrows mark experience flowing back
into memory across tasks and deployments.

### 5.1 Lifelong Personalization and Companion Agents

Personal agents must adapt as users change. Over time, schedules shift,
learners build skills or form misconceptions, health symptoms evolve,
and interpersonal bonds deepen. To remain useful, an agent must update
its internal model of the user and use this state to guide dialogue,
planning, and tool calls.

Personal assistants. Assistants use cross-session logs to maintain
conversational continuity and match individual style. Retrieving user
profile items directly improves generation on tasks like email drafting
and news summarization \[[Salemi et al., 2024](#bib.bib66)\], while
modeling complete interaction histories captures personal tone and
habits \[[Liu et al., 2025b](#bib.bib67)\]. Crucially, assistants must
track when facts expire. Recent systems update user facts across
conversations \[[Bae et al., 2022](#bib.bib68)\], decay memory strength
over time \[[Zhong et al., 2024](#bib.bib100)\], record user feedback
alongside its valid context \[[Yuan et al., 2025b](#bib.bib69)\], and
organize changing user states through causal links \[[Ong et al.,
2025](#bib.bib70)\]. These mechanisms ensure that assistants preserve
core preferences while promptly reflecting new changes.

Tutoring agents. Tutoring agents track what a student knows and where
they struggle. For example, ScaffoldLM stores pedagogical goals,
step-level progress, and inferred misconceptions in an assessment
memory, selecting the next hint based on the learner’s latest response
\[[Li et al., 2026b](#bib.bib71)\]. Similarly, versioned student
profiles track skill mastery, error patterns, and review schedules to
calibrate hints and spaced practice \[[David and Ghosh,
2026](#bib.bib72)\]. Updating student profiles in real time allows
agents to adjust lesson pacing, hint specificity, and problem difficulty
on the fly.

Health follow-up and emotional companionship. Memory directly underpins
long-term care and companionship. In a ten-week study of 147 real-world
users (1,252 calls), the CareCall system used memory to revisit past
health complaints and life events. This continuity encouraged users to
disclose more health details and build trust \[[Jo et al.,
2024](#bib.bib73)\]. Studies on Replika users show that social bonding
varies widely in depth and pace, meaning companion agents cannot rely on
static facts alone \[[Skjuve et al., 2022](#bib.bib74), [Skjuve et al.,
2023](#bib.bib75)\]. Effective companionship requires referencing shared
moments and conversational routines. For instance, recent agents infer
implicit emotional needs and retrieve relevant shared memories to offer
proactive comfort \[[Fu et al., 2026](#bib.bib76)\].

From personalized generation to tool use. Personalization extends beyond
text generation to planning and tool execution. Dual-reflection
mechanisms build user memory banks that accurately route past
preferences to current prompts \[[Tan et al., 2025b](#bib.bib53)\]. In
tool-augmented setups, benchmarks like Mem2ActBench test whether agents
can translate past dates, locations, and habits into correct API
parameters \[[Shen et al., 2026](#bib.bib77)\]. Through memory,
personalization becomes a continuous feedback loop: each interaction
updates the user model, which in turn steers the agent’s next action.

### 5.2 Evolving Problem-Solving in Long-Horizon Digital Environments

Digital tasks often span multiple web pages, applications, and execution
steps. Web research requires tracking checked links and active filters;
desktop tasks coordinate code editors, terminals, and spreadsheets; and
software engineering requires navigating evolving codebases. In these
settings, continual learning serves two needs: resuming interrupted
workflows and transferring problem-solving strategies across tasks.
Memory tracks progress to ensure task continuity, while abstracting
successful runs into reusable skills and debugging routines.

Web automation. Web navigation offers an ideal testbed for workflow
reuse. For example, AWM distills reusable workflows from successful web
trajectories and feeds them into future runs \[[Wang et al.,
2025c](#bib.bib39)\]. Across 200 domains (such as shopping and travel),
this approach raised success rates by 24.6% on Mind2Web and 51.1% on
WebArena while cutting overall action steps. Online-learned
routines—like filtering products or comparing flight routes—readily
transfer to new websites with similar structures.

Desktop and cross-application tasks. Desktop agents require both state
tracking and skill reuse across applications. Systems like OS-Copilot
store verified tool scripts in procedural memory \[[Wu et al.,
2024b](#bib.bib78)\], while other architectures combine past execution
traces with hierarchical planning to navigate unfamiliar interfaces
\[[Agashe et al., 2024](#bib.bib79)\]. Similarly, Trace2Skill distills
raw trajectories into executable skill documents, demonstrating reuse
across spreadsheet operations, vision-language tasks, and math reasoning
\[[Ni et al., 2026](#bib.bib80)\]. For task continuity, OpenHands logs
commands, file diffs, browser events, and user inputs into a unified
event stream \[[Wang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib81)\]. This complete history
lets coding agents resume interrupted jobs without losing context.
Together, these systems show that combining execution logs with
procedural skills supports complex, multi-app workflows.

Software engineering. Software engineering scales these demands to
entire codebases. Coding agents must understand repository structures
while logging inspected files, failed patches, and unit test outputs.
SWEMem uses issue progress to selectively compress context, keeping
context windows manageable during extended debugging sessions \[[Gao et
al., 2026](#bib.bib82)\]. To transfer knowledge across bugs, SWE-Exp
extracts debugging strategies from both successful and failed repairs to
guide new GitHub issues \[[Chen et al., 2025](#bib.bib83)\]. Thus,
memory maintains local focus within a single bug fix while transferring
repair strategies across the repository.

Experience distillation and strategy reuse. Through trial and error,
digital agents turn raw execution traces into reusable strategies.
Recent frameworks extract high-level rules from repeated attempts,
attach clear trigger conditions to guidelines, and index strategies
derived from both successes and failures \[[Zhao et al.,
2024a](#bib.bib34), [Fu et al., 2024](#bib.bib84), [Ouyang et al.,
2026](#bib.bib40)\]. Terminal outputs, browser states, and test suites
provide immediate feedback to test whether an extracted rule works. In
short, memory gives digital agents two capabilities: it keeps
long-horizon jobs from derailing, and it turns past mistakes into faster
future execution.

### 5.3 Continuous Exploration in Embodied and Open Worlds

Embodied agents must track a dynamic physical world while expanding
their motor skills. Robots navigating homes must remember shifted
furniture; robotic arms must adapt to unfamiliar tools; and open-world
agents must set new goals as they explore. These tasks require two
memory systems: a world-state memory that updates with visual
observations, and a skill memory that stores reusable behaviors.
Together, they allow agents to map their surroundings and master complex
actions.

Long-term navigation and scene understanding. Mobile robots rely on
spatial memory to navigate and track dynamic scenes. For example,
RememBR indexes video clips with spatial and temporal tags, allowing
robots to answer queries about past object locations during navigation
\[[Anwar et al., 2025](#bib.bib85)\]. Similarly, EmbodiedVideoAgent
combines egocentric video, depth, and pose data into an object-centric
memory, updating object states as interactions occur \[[Fan et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib86)\]. This structured memory boosts benchmark scores on
Ego4D-VQ3D, OpenEQA, and EnvQA, and directly guides grasping,
navigation, and placement. Historical observations thus become an
editable 3D world model.

Open-world exploration. In open worlds like Minecraft, agents must learn
complex behaviors without human supervision. Voyager implements this by
proposing new tasks, writing executable skill code, and saving verified
skills into a library \[[Wang et al., 2024b](#bib.bib38)\]. This skill
bank helped Voyager discover 3.3$`\times`$ more items and unlock
tech-tree milestones 15.3$`\times`$ faster than baselines. Crucially,
the saved skill library lets agents solve unseen tasks from scratch in
brand-new worlds. In text worlds like ScienceWorld, CLIN abstracts
action outcomes into causal rules to guide future planning \[[Majumder
et al., 2023](#bib.bib87)\]. By saving executable code and causal
patterns, exploration continually feeds subsequent skill acquisition.

Cross-task and cross-embodiment adaptation. Manipulating physical
objects requires sample-efficient learning. RoboCat addresses this by
pooling experience across different simulated and real robot arms
\[[Bousmalis et al., 2024](#bib.bib88)\]. The model learns a generalist
policy, generates self-supervised data on new tasks, and feeds these
runs back into training. Across this self-improving training loop,
success rates on unseen tasks (after fine-tuning on 500 demonstrations)
rose from 36% with the initial model to 74% with the final RoboCat
model. Pooling data across diverse robot hardware shows that
cross-embodiment memory drastically cuts the demonstrations needed for
new tasks.

Robot fleets. Robot fleets turn shared deployment into collective policy
updates. In a 16-robot setup, autonomous trajectories and human
teleoperation interventions are pooled to train a single generalist
policy, which is then flashed back to the fleet \[[Wang et al.,
2026c](#bib.bib89)\]. Across eight manipulation tasks—including grocery
restocking routines lasting 3–5 minutes—accumulated fleet data pushed
success rates to 95%. Field deployment thus acts as a continual training
engine, linking individual robot trials to fleet-wide policy
improvements.

### 5.4 Collaborative Evolution via Shared Multi-Agent Memory

Multi-agent systems require shared memory to coordinate specialized
roles. Software teams must log architectural decisions; data teams need
to track data provenance; and scientific teams must record hypotheses,
lab results, and reviews. Storing these records in a shared memory pool
prevents redundant effort and lets agents reuse established workflows
and team roles. Memory acts as the team’s shared workspace, linking past
findings to current tasks.

Cross-task collaboration procedures. Shared procedural memory allows
teams to reuse proven collaboration patterns. G-Memory organizes
multi-agent interaction histories into a three-tier graph of dialogues,
queries, and insights, letting agents retrieve both raw traces and
abstract lessons \[[Zhang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib90)\]. Across five
benchmarks, this structured memory improved embodied task success and
question-answering accuracy. Similarly, LegoMem splits memory into
orchestrator and worker tiers: orchestrator memory sharpens task
decomposition, while worker memory improves tool-use accuracy \[[Han et
al., 2025](#bib.bib91)\]. Reusing execution traces even helps teams of
smaller LLMs match the performance of proprietary models.

Member expertise and selective sharing. Teams also benefit from
remembering which agent is good at what. LLMA-Mem builds a transactive
memory that models member expertise, team layouts, and past coordination
to optimize future role assignment \[[Wu et al., 2026b](#bib.bib92)\].
But sharing everything overwhelms agents. To prevent broadcast clutter,
Fioresi et al. train an admission policy that only writes an
intermediate step to global memory if it benefits downstream agents
\[[Fioresi et al., 2026](#bib.bib93)\]. On AssistantBench and GAIA, this
selective sharing cut duplicated work without hurting task accuracy. In
short, memory decides who should do the work and what information is
worth broadcasting.

Scientific collaboration. Scientific discovery teams use shared memory
to iterate on complex research programs. In VirtualLab, specialized AI
scientists hold multi-round meetings around a shared workspace to design
nanobodies, guided by a human lead \[[Swanson et al.,
2025](#bib.bib94)\]. Co-Scientist refines hypotheses across cycles of
generation, reflection, and peer review, using persistent context memory
to carry validation results forward \[[Gottweis et al.,
2026](#bib.bib95)\]. Similarly, Robin connects literature search, lab
protocol design, and data analysis into an autonomous loop where wet-lab
results update current hypotheses \[[Ghareeb et al.,
2026](#bib.bib96)\]. Shared memory keeps these long-running research
pipelines coherent across iterative design cycles.

Synthesis of shared-memory applications. Effective teamwork relies on
three forms of continuity: project state (what the team is doing),
member profiles (who is best equipped to do it), and shared procedures
(how the work was done before). Project state keeps ongoing
investigations on track; member profiles streamline role assignments;
and reusable workflows eliminate redundant trial and error. Shared
memory turns individual agent outputs into lasting organizational
assets.

Cross-domain synthesis. Together, these four domains highlight the
practical scope of memory-centric continual learning. Applications now
span multi-week health companions, cross-application web agents,
repository-level bug fixing, open-world discovery, fleet-scale robotics,
and automated science labs. In each case, memory links past experience
to future action. By retaining and organizing execution traces, systems
avoid repeating mistakes, adapt to changing users and environments, and
continually turn raw interactions into durable capabilities.

![Refer to caption](2610.29357v1/memory_challenges_landscape.png)

Figure 15: Four open challenges in memory-centric continual learning:
(1) credit assignment across prospective, retrospective, and long-term
horizons; (2) the stability–plasticity dilemma driven by memory
interference; (3) a bridge connecting episodic experience with
parametric weights; and (4) a lifecycle pipeline of deployment risks and
governance rules. Colors distinguish the four challenges, and the bottom
bar highlights their core questions.

## 6 Open Challenges in Memory-Centric Continual Learning

### 6.1 Predicting Experience Value and Long-Term Credit Assignment

Current agents still rely on handcrafted heuristics rather than learned
policies $`\pi^{\mathrm{mem}}`$. The main obstacle is delayed credit
assignment. When an agent records an event at step $`t`$, it must decide
three things: whether to save it, how to format it, and what metadata to
keep. But the true value of this write may not show up until step
$`t+100`$ or later, after many rounds of summarization, pruning, and
task shifts. As a result, final task success reflects a long chain of
memory actions, not a single write. This delay blurs the credit signal.
Agents can easily reflect using prompts, but they struggle to train
policies that decide what to keep, update, or delete [Bhargava and
Barrento \[2026\]](#bib.bib134), [Yan et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib135), [Yu
et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib56). Feedback simply changes memory content
($`\mathcal{M}_{t}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{t+1}`$), while the policy
itself stays fixed
($`\pi_{t}^{\mathrm{mem}}=\pi_{t+1}^{\mathrm{mem}}`$).

This challenge grows as memory moves from raw chat logs to reusable
experience. Storing everything is impractical. Agents must weigh the
future payoff of an experience against storage size, search latency, and
the risk of confusion [Luo et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib128), [Huang et al.
\[2026\]](#bib.bib1), [Shu et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib110), [Zhang et al.
\[2026c\]](#bib.bib138), [Wu et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib139). Recent
surveys map out how memory is written, managed, and read [Du
\[2026\]](#bib.bib136), [Wu et al. \[2026c\]](#bib.bib137), [Huang et
al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib1), [Zhoubian et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib2), [Jiang
et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib140). Yet we still lack a principled way to
balance these costs and benefits over time. Most existing methods rely
on simple shortcuts: (1) rules based on recency, frequency, or text
similarity [Luo et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib128), [Wu et al.
\[2026c\]](#bib.bib137); (2) learning to call tools like store, search,
or summarize [Yu et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib56), [Zhang et al.
\[2026b\]](#bib.bib127), [Zhang et al. \[2025b\]](#bib.bib6), [Wang et
al. \[2025b\]](#bib.bib122); or (3) hard budgets on context size [Zhang
et al. \[2026c\]](#bib.bib138), [Wu et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib139),
[Zhoubian et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib2).

These shortcuts only look at the current prompt or immediate reward.
They ignore delayed returns, distribution shifts, and interactions
between co-retrieved memories. Saving bad data pollutes the store,
triggers negative transfer, and hurts future tasks [Shu et al.
\[2026\]](#bib.bib110), [Wei et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib54), [Hu et al.
\[2026a\]](#bib.bib115), [Yang et al. \[2026c\]](#bib.bib118), [Yu et
al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib109), [Wu et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib139). Three
practical hurdles prevent delayed rewards from training
$`\pi^{\mathrm{mem}}`$:

- •
  Predicting value before writing. At write time, the agent does not
  know future queries or tasks. Dropping an experience loses information
  permanently. Keeping bad data pollutes the store. The policy must
  forecast long-term utility using only local, present clues [Bhargava
  and Barrento \[2026\]](#bib.bib134), [Du \[2026\]](#bib.bib136), [Shu
  et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib110), [Wu et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib139).
- •
  Tracing failure back through the pipeline. Final errors stem from many
  sources: poor admission, lossy summarization, bad retrieval, or weak
  reasoning. When an agent fails, it is hard to isolate whether memory
  or reasoning caused the error [Bhargava and Barrento
  \[2026\]](#bib.bib134), [Jiang et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib140), [Du
  \[2026\]](#bib.bib136), [Ai et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib113), [Yan et al.
  \[2026\]](#bib.bib135), [Zhang et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib61).
- •
  Path dependence across runs. Every write or delete alters the memory
  state for future sessions. Different rollouts no longer share the same
  environment. This variance breaks standard reinforcement learning
  assumptions and destabilizes training [Yan et al.
  \[2026\]](#bib.bib135), [Yang et al. \[2026c\]](#bib.bib118), [Yu et
  al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib56), [Yu et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib109), [Zhang
  et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib61).

Solving these hurdles requires optimizing for long-term net value. For
write-time prediction, agents can learn value models on task streams,
using scaling curves to judge marginal returns [Bhargava and Barrento
\[2026\]](#bib.bib134), [Shu et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib110), [Zhoubian et
al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib2), [Xiao et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib142). To trace
errors, systems can log write decisions, run ablation tests, and keep
links from summaries back to raw source episodes [Bhargava and Barrento
\[2026\]](#bib.bib134), [Du \[2026\]](#bib.bib136), [Jiang et al.
\[2026\]](#bib.bib140), [Shu et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib110). To handle
path dependence, agents can branch rollouts from identical memory
checkpoints and use hindsight critics [Yan et al.
\[2026\]](#bib.bib135), [Tan et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib141), [Yu et al.
\[2026b\]](#bib.bib56), [Zhang et al. \[2026c\]](#bib.bib138), [Wu et
al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib139). The ultimate goal is closed-loop control:
task feedback should update not just what the agent remembers, but how
it manages memory.

### 6.2 The Stability–Plasticity Dilemma in Dynamic Environments
````
