External memory gives agents a way to learn continually at the system
level. Like classic continual learning, it aims to acquire new
capabilities, preserve old knowledge, and transfer skills [Luo et al.
\[2026\]](#bib.bib128), [Xiong et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib129). As noted
in Section
[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Memory Substrates: Physical Foundations for Stability and Plasticity ‣ 2 A Unified Framework: Architecting Memory for Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"),
parametric learning updates model weights, while agent memory updates
external records at inference time. However, external stores do not
remove the stability–plasticity dilemma; they simply relocate it [Hu et
al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib115).

Here, interference occurs during retrieval and prompt construction. A
loose write policy (Section
[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Formation and Selective Writing ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))
boosts plasticity by saving new records quickly, but it also clutters
storage with duplicate and conflicting entries. Under fixed retrieval
budgets, this clutter crowds out useful memories. In the cascade of
Section
[4.1.1](#S4.SS1.SSS1 "4.1.1 Retention: Learning Must Survive Delay and Interference ‣ 4.1 What to Measure: Retention, Adaptation, and Transfer ‣ 4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"),
saving more records (Gate 1) hurts retrieval accuracy (Gate 2). Even
when retrieved, outdated facts can lead the model to ignore correct
updates or apply old rules to wrong tasks (Gate 3) [Hu et al.
\[2026a\]](#bib.bib115), [Liang et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib130), [Kim et
al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib131), [Zhang et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib61).
Stability means keeping valid memories easy to find and use. Plasticity
means updating old facts without breaking what already works.

This trade-off sharpens as memory expands. More interactions can help
agents learn [Ouyang et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib40), and periodic cleaning
keeps stores lean [Yin et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib132). Yet larger stores
always raise the risk of retrieval noise. We should judge memory scaling
by task gains under fixed compute budgets, not by raw storage volume.

Measuring these trade-offs requires long-term benchmarks. Recent
benchmarks test specific skills, such as fact updates in LongMemEval [Wu
et al. \[2025a\]](#bib.bib98) or task transfer in Evo-Memory and
LifelongAgentBench [Wei et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib54), [Zheng et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib103). As outlined in Section
[4](#S4 "4 Evaluating Memory-Augmented Agents as Continual Learners ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"),
evaluations should track retention, forward transfer, and negative
interference across shifting task streams. Beyond final accuracy,
tracking survival rates, retrieval ranks, and actual memory usage
against frozen-memory baselines shows whether memory truly helps or
quietly harms performance.

The core question is direct: *How can agents keep memories accurate and
relevant as experience grows?* Promising paths include write filters
that prevent clutter, consolidation that checks raw evidence, and
retrieval that matches the exact conditions where an experience applies
[Hu et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib115), [Zhang et al.
\[2026a\]](#bib.bib61), [Liang et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib130).

### 6.3 Synergistic Continual Learning through Experience Memory and Parametric Learning

Continual learning requires coordinating external experience with
internal model weights. Today, agents treat them as two separate
systems. External memory stores new facts quickly without touching model
weights [Shinn et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib15), [Chhikara et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib17), [Xu et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib18). Parametric
fine-tuning bakes patterns into weights for broad generalization, but
risks catastrophic forgetting [Wu et al. \[2024a\]](#bib.bib143), [Shi
et al. \[2025\]](#bib.bib144).

Recent work connects the two sides by extracting reasoning paths into
external memory or fine-tuning lightweight adapters [Gutiérrez et al.
\[2025\]](#bib.bib19), [Ouyang et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib40), [Zhao et
al. \[2024b\]](#bib.bib145), [He et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib146). Yet
information flows mostly one way: from raw episodes into weights or
static stores. Systems cannot easily edit, roll back, or delete
parametric knowledge once written. Human memory works differently. In
biology, recalling a consolidated memory makes it flexible again,
meaning the brain must actively restabilize it (a process called
reconsolidation) [Nader et al. \[2000\]](#bib.bib158). Neural networks
lack this feature; once weights change, they stay fixed until the next
gradient step.

The key missing piece is the bridge between raw experience and
parametric weights. An agent should first test and summarize experiences
in external storage. Only repeatedly verified patterns should enter
model weights. Conversely, when existing weights fail on new evidence,
the agent must pull that knowledge back into explicit text to inspect
and fix it. This cycle follows Complementary Learning Systems (CLS)
theory: fast learning in the hippocampus and slow integration in the
cortex interact via replay to balance plasticity and stability
[McClelland et al. \[1995\]](#bib.bib147), [Kumaran et al.
\[2016\]](#bib.bib148), [Wilson and McNaughton \[1994\]](#bib.bib159).
Furthermore, the brain does not store every event; synaptic
tagging-and-capture shows that novelty and reward flags decide which
synapses undergo lasting change [Frey and Morris \[1997\]](#bib.bib160).

Future systems should build a two-way learning loop across three clear
steps:

1.  1.
    *Selection*: Filter raw traces by novelty, utility, and
    frequency—like synaptic tagging—before keeping them.
2.  2.
    *Consolidation*: Replay, abstract, and bake verified memories into
    model weights via modular adapters.
3.  3.
    *Revision*: Test weights against new facts, reopen outdated
    knowledge for correction (like reconsolidation), and use schemas to
    absorb new rules quickly [Tse et al. \[2007\]](#bib.bib161).

Instead of treating memory and weights as separate silos, agents need
clear algorithms that pass knowledge back and forth between them.

### 6.4 Trustworthy Memory Governance in Lifelong Deployment

While methods for writing and searching memory have matured, memory
safety and control lag behind. Few studies address access control,
rollback, or secure deletion, even though real-world deployment risks
center on these exact areas [Ding et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib133).

Without explicit governance, security risks spread across every stage of
the memory lifecycle:

- •
  Formation: Attackers can inject malicious text through poisoned web
  pages or prompt injection. Without write checks, agents save these
  instructions as trusted facts, corrupting future actions (Section
  [3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Formation and Selective Writing ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))
  [Chen et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib151), [Dong et al.
  \[2025\]](#bib.bib152), [Tian et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib153).
- •
  Consolidation: When agents summarize logs into general rules, they
  often drop timestamps and source links—a failure known as context
  collapse (Section
  [3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Organization, Compression, and Consolidation ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
  As a result, a single poisoned prompt can turn into a permanent,
  trusted behavioral rule.
- •
  Retrieval: Corrupted memories can hijack tool calls and override user
  instructions during normal tasks [Xu et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib154),
  or leak private data across users in shared stores [Yang et al.
  \[2026a\]](#bib.bib155). This shows why retrieving a memory is
  different from using it safely (Section
  [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Retrieval and Utilization ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents")).
- •
  Forgetting: Deleting a raw record does not remove its downstream
  traces, such as summaries, fine-tuned weights, or knowledge graphs
  (Section
  [3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Revision and Forgetting ‣ 3 The Memory Lifecycle as the Engine of Continual Learning ‣ Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents"))
  [Lin et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib149). Bad behavior can persist long
  after the original entry is gone [Ding et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib133).

Memory control is hard for three reasons: (1) *Expanding scope*: As
agents gain more tools and autonomy, attack surfaces constantly shift
[Zhou et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib150). (2) *Delayed impact*: The gap
between a poisoned write and an attack can be weeks or months.
Summarization hides original sources, making it hard to trace who wrote
what [Lin et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib149). (3) *Direct trade-offs*: Strict
privacy checks and audit logs slow down search and increase token costs
[Lam et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib157).

Recent work introduces basic defenses: write permissions, provenance
logs, user-isolated retrieval, and verified rollback [Lin et al.
\[2026\]](#bib.bib149). Yet evaluation still falls short. Most
benchmarks only score task success, giving developers little reason to
build safe memory systems [Ding et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib133). In
addition, self-reflection remains hard to verify from the outside, and
shared memory in multi-agent setups lacks clear access rules [Wang et
al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib156), [Yang et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib155).

To build reliable agents, governance must cover the full lifecycle:

- •
  *Formation*: Verify sources and permissions before writing, rather
  than filtering data after problems occur [Lin et al.
  \[2026\]](#bib.bib149).
- •
  *Consolidation*: Borrow ideas from database recovery and belief
  revision to prevent agents from dropping critical source context
  during summarization [Ding et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib133).
- •
  *Retrieval*: Sandbox tool execution and enforce role-based access for
  shared multi-agent stores [Wang et al. \[2026b\]](#bib.bib156), [Yang
  et al. \[2026a\]](#bib.bib155).
- •
  *Forgetting*: Use clear metrics—such as stress tests and recovery
  targets—to prove that deleted data and its side effects are truly gone
  [Ding et al. \[2026\]](#bib.bib133).

Governance cannot remain an afterthought; it must become a testable,
built-in guarantee of agent systems.

## 7 Conclusion

In this paper, we reframe agent memory as an evolving learning
infrastructure for continual learning, rather than a passive storage or
retrieval component. We argue that experience accumulation cannot be
equated with static recall, but requires a closed-loop dynamic of
retention, adaptation, and transfer, explaining why current memory
agents fail under delay, interference, and environmental drift. To
address this, we establish a unified framework spanning physical
substrates, reusable assets, and lifecycle policies, coupled with a
behavior-centric evaluation methodology to isolate genuine experiential
gains and a memory scaling law that relates those gains to experience
and memory resources. By adopting this memory-centric lens, the field
shifts its central question from whether an agent can remember to
whether it can continually learn, paving a more principled path towards
building resilient, self-improving intelligent systems.
